Question 12Skipped

An automotive manufacturer, DriveCore Systems, is running containerized ML applications to process operational sensor data from assembly lines and generate predictive maintenance insights for production equipment.

These applications are deployed across Amazon EC2 instances, an Amazon Elastic Container Service (Amazon ECS) cluster, and AWS Lambda functions.

The EC2 and ECS workloads store predictions and artifacts on Amazon Elastic Block Store (Amazon EBS) volumes. To optimize costs, DriveCore Systems wants to identify inefficiently used resources and receive actionable recommendations to reduce expenses. 

What is the most efficient solution to achieve this goal with minimal development effort?

Use AWS Budgets to analyze the specifications and utilization metrics of DriveCore Systems' AWS resources
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Budgets is primarily used to set cost and usage thresholds, monitor Reserved Instance (RI) or Savings Plans coverage, and trigger alerts or automated actions when spending exceeds defined limits. It does not analyze resource-level specifications or utilization metrics to produce rightsizing recommendations for EC2 instances, ECS tasks, Lambda functions, or EBS volumes. For a scenario like DriveCore Systems' — where the goal is actionable optimization guidance across multiple compute resource types — AWS Compute Optimizer is the appropriate service. Keyword tip: when a question mentions 'budget alerts,' 'spending thresholds,' or 'RI coverage monitoring,' think AWS Budgets — not resource-level rightsizing.
Correct answer
Use AWS Compute Optimizer to analyze the specifications and utilization metrics of DriveCore Systems' AWS compute resources
Explanation
Correct. AWS Compute Optimizer analyzes the configuration and utilization metrics of AWS compute resources — including Amazon EC2 instances, Amazon ECS services on AWS Fargate, AWS Lambda functions, Amazon EBS volumes, and Amazon RDS — and generates rightsizing recommendations with no custom development required. It provides dashboard-level savings opportunity metrics and performance improvement opportunity metrics, allowing teams such as DriveCore Systems' engineering staff to quickly prioritize optimization actions across accounts and resource types. This directly addresses the requirement to identify inefficiently used resources and receive actionable cost-reduction recommendations with minimal effort. Keyword tip: whenever a question mentions rightsizing across EC2, ECS, Lambda, or EBS with actionable per-resource recommendations and minimal development effort, AWS Compute Optimizer is the correct answer.
Use AWS Cost Explorer to analyze the specifications and utilization metrics of DriveCore Systems' AWS compute resources
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Cost Explorer provides visualization and analysis of AWS costs and usage over time through custom reports and trend identification. While it surfaces some rightsizing recommendations for EC2, it integrates with AWS Compute Optimizer to retrieve those recommendations rather than generating them natively. Cost Explorer is best suited for understanding historical spend patterns and identifying cost drivers at an aggregate level, not for deep per-resource utilization-based rightsizing across EC2, ECS, Lambda, and EBS simultaneously. Keyword tip: when the scenario asks for 'cost visualization,' 'spend trend analysis,' or 'historical usage reports,' think AWS Cost Explorer — but for resource-level rightsizing recommendations, think AWS Compute Optimizer.
Use AWS Trusted Advisor to analyze the specifications and utilization metrics of DriveCore Systems' AWS resources
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Trusted Advisor inspects the AWS environment against best practices across five pillars — cost optimization, performance, security, fault tolerance, and service limits — and provides broad, high-level recommendations. While it includes some cost optimization checks, it does not perform deep utilization-metric analysis for specific compute resource types such as EBS volumes or ECS tasks to the same granularity that AWS Compute Optimizer provides. Trusted Advisor is best used for general account health reviews and broad best-practice compliance rather than targeted compute rightsizing. Keyword tip: when a question references 'overall account health,' 'best practice checks across multiple pillars,' or 'service limit reviews,' think AWS Trusted Advisor — not granular compute rightsizing.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the ability to select the correct AWS cost-optimization service for a scenario involving multiple compute resource types, including Amazon EC2, Amazon ECS, AWS Lambda, and Amazon EBS.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Use AWS Compute Optimizer to analyze the specifications and utilization metrics of DriveCore Systems' AWS compute resources: AWS Compute Optimizer analyzes the configuration and utilization metrics of AWS compute resources, including Amazon EC2 instances, Amazon ECS services on AWS Fargate, AWS Lambda functions, Amazon EBS volumes, and Amazon RDS. It generates rightsizing recommendations to help reduce cost and improve performance.

    Use AWS Budgets to analyze the specifications and utilization metrics of DriveCore Systems' AWS resources: AWS Budgets is primarily used to set cost and usage thresholds, monitor Reserved Instance or Savings Plans coverage, and trigger alerts or automated actions when spending exceeds defined limits. It does not provide detailed per-resource rightsizing recommendations based on utilization metrics.

    Use AWS Cost Explorer to analyze the specifications and utilization metrics of DriveCore Systems' AWS compute resources: AWS Cost Explorer provides visualization and analysis of AWS costs and usage over time through custom reports and trend identification. It is useful for understanding spending patterns, but it does not provide the same actionable rightsizing recommendations across compute resources as AWS Compute Optimizer.

    Use AWS Trusted Advisor to analyze the specifications and utilization metrics of DriveCore Systems' AWS resources: AWS Trusted Advisor inspects the AWS environment against best practices across cost optimization, performance, security, fault tolerance, and service quotas. It provides broad recommendations, but it is less specialized than AWS Compute Optimizer for resource-level compute rightsizing.

Why AWS Compute Optimizer fits this scenario.

AWS Compute Optimizer directly addresses the requirement to identify inefficiently used compute resources and receive actionable cost-reduction recommendations with minimal effort.

It evaluates historical utilization metrics and configuration data to recommend better resource choices, such as smaller EC2 instance types, optimized EBS volume configurations, improved Lambda memory settings, and better ECS on Fargate sizing.

This makes it the best fit for an industrial ML workload such as DriveCore Systems' predictive maintenance platform, where multiple compute services may be overprovisioned or inefficiently configured.

Exam tip.

Whenever a question mentions rightsizing across EC2, ECS, Lambda, or EBS with actionable per-resource recommendations and minimal development effort, AWS Compute Optimizer is the correct answer.

Remember:

    Per-resource rightsizing recommendations: Use AWS Compute Optimizer.

    Budget thresholds and spend alerts: Use AWS Budgets.

    Historical cost analysis and cost trends: Use AWS Cost Explorer.

    Broad best-practice checks: Use AWS Trusted Advisor.

Official AWS references.

    AWS Compute Optimizer FAQs.

    What is AWS Compute Optimizer?

Key exam terms: AWS Compute Optimizer, rightsizing recommendations, EC2 utilization metrics, EBS volume optimization, ECS on Fargate, Lambda rightsizing, minimal development effort, cost optimization without custom tooling.

ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security
Question 13Skipped

A healthcare diagnostics provider, MedVision Analytics, has tasked its ML team to analyze patient transaction and diagnostic records for enhancing its fraud detection system and identifying potential patient segments for targeted outreach campaigns.

The company has a dataset with multiple features, including diagnostic billing amounts, visit frequency, and facility location data.  The tasks are listed below, and the ML team must use Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithms to complete these tasks:

- Reduce the dimensionality of the dataset to improve model performance and visualization. - Perform cluster analysis to identify distinct patient groups.

- Detect anomalous billing transactions that may indicate fraud.  Which combination of SageMaker built-in algorithms should the ML team use to meet the requirements? 
Linear Learner for dimensionality reduction, DBSCAN for cluster analysis, and Random Cut Forest (RCF) for anomaly detection
Explanation
Incorrect. The Amazon SageMaker Linear Learner algorithm is a supervised learning algorithm designed for classification and regression tasks, not for dimensionality reduction. Additionally, DBSCAN (Density-Based Spatial Clustering of Applications with Noise) is not available as a SageMaker built-in algorithm; it exists only in open-source frameworks such as scikit-learn. Selecting algorithms outside the SageMaker built-in algorithm library directly violates the stated requirement that only built-in algorithms be used. Keyword tip: DBSCAN and other density-based clustering algorithms are common exam distractors — always verify that any algorithm named in an answer option is an officially supported SageMaker built-in before selecting it.
Correct answer
Principal Component Analysis (PCA) for dimensionality reduction, K-Means for cluster analysis, and Random Cut Forest (RCF) for anomaly detection
Explanation
Correct. This combination correctly addresses all three requirements using exclusively Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithms. PCA (Principal Component Analysis) is an unsupervised algorithm that reduces dataset dimensionality by identifying a new set of uncorrelated components that capture maximum variance, improving both model performance and visualization. K-Means is an unsupervised clustering algorithm that partitions data points into k groups so that intra-cluster similarity is maximized and inter-cluster similarity is minimized, making it well-suited for identifying distinct patient segments. Random Cut Forest (RCF) is an unsupervised anomaly detection algorithm that assigns an anomaly score to each data point based on its isolation within random subspace partitions, making it ideal for flagging unusual billing transactions indicative of fraud. Keyword tip: PCA + K-Means + RCF is the canonical SageMaker built-in combination for the triad of dimensionality reduction, unsupervised clustering, and anomaly detection — recognize this pattern in any scenario requiring all three tasks with SageMaker native algorithms.
XGBoost for dimensionality reduction, K-Means for cluster analysis, and Random Cut Forest (RCF) for anomaly detection
Explanation
Incorrect. XGBoost is an implementation of the gradient-boosted trees algorithm designed exclusively for supervised learning tasks such as binary classification, multiclass classification, regression, and ranking. It has no capability for dimensionality reduction, which requires an unsupervised feature transformation method such as PCA. Substituting XGBoost in place of PCA for this task reflects a fundamental misunderstanding of its purpose within the SageMaker built-in algorithm library. Keyword tip: whenever a question specifies dimensionality reduction as a requirement, XGBoost is always a distractor — it is a supervised, tree-based algorithm, not a feature transformation or unsupervised method.
Principal Component Analysis (PCA) for dimensionality reduction, K-Means for cluster analysis, and Neural Networks for anomaly detection
Explanation
Incorrect. While PCA and K-Means are appropriate SageMaker built-in choices for dimensionality reduction and cluster analysis respectively, Neural Networks are not a SageMaker built-in algorithm for anomaly detection. The correct SageMaker built-in algorithm for detecting anomalous data points is Random Cut Forest (RCF), which operates unsupervised and requires no labeled fraud examples. Deep learning approaches using neural networks or autoencoders require custom containers or SageMaker's managed framework environments (e.g., TensorFlow, MXNet) and are not appropriate for fulfilling an anomaly detection requirement within the built-in algorithm ecosystem. Keyword tip: for anomaly detection using SageMaker built-in algorithms, always select RCF — Neural Networks and Autoencoders are distractors in this context, particularly when the question explicitly constrains the solution to SageMaker native built-ins.
Overall explanation

What this question tests

This question tests knowledge of the Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithm library and the ability to map three distinct ML tasks , dimensionality reduction, clustering, and anomaly detection , to the correct unsupervised algorithms available natively in SageMaker. It is tied to the ML Model Development domain. 

The concepts at a glance

- Principal Component Analysis (PCA) for dimensionality reduction, K-Means for cluster analysis, and Random Cut Forest (RCF) for anomaly detection: This combination correctly addresses all three requirements using exclusively Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithms.

- XGBoost for dimensionality reduction, K-Means for cluster analysis, and Random Cut Forest (RCF) for anomaly detection: XGBoost is an implementation of the gradient-boosted trees algorithm designed exclusively for supervised learning tasks such as binary classification, multiclass classification, regression, and ranking. - Linear Learner for dimensionality reduction, DBSCAN for cluster analysis, and Random Cut Forest (RCF) for anomaly detection: The Amazon SageMaker Linear Learner algorithm is a supervised learning algorithm designed for classification and regression tasks, not for dimensionality reduction.

- Principal Component Analysis (PCA) for dimensionality reduction, K-Means for cluster analysis, and Neural Networks for anomaly detection: While PCA and K-Means are appropriate SageMaker built-in choices for dimensionality reduction and cluster analysis respectively, Neural Networks are not a SageMaker built-in algorithm for anomaly detection.  Why Principal Component Analysis (PCA) for dimensionality reduction, K-Means for cluster analysis, and Random Cut Forest (RCF) for anomaly detection fits this scenario This combination correctly addresses all three requirements using exclusively Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithms. K-Means is an unsupervised clustering algorithm that partitions data points into k groups so that intra-cluster similarity is maximized and inter-cluster similarity is minimized, making it well-suited for identifying distinct patient segments. 

Exam tip PCA + K-Means + RCF is the canonical SageMaker built-in combination for the triad of dimensionality reduction, unsupervised clustering, and anomaly detection , recognize this pattern in any scenario requiring all three tasks with SageMaker native algorithms. 


Key exam terms: SageMaker built-in algorithms, Principal Component Analysis (PCA), K-Means clustering, Random Cut Forest (RCF), dimensionality reduction, unsupervised learning, anomaly detection, DBSCAN distractor

Question 14Skipped

A commercial real estate firm, Meridian Property Group, is developing an ML model to predict property maintenance intervals across its managed portfolio.


The dataset contains tabular data with ordered features, such as inspection dates and service billing amounts, alongside sensitive personnel information like employee identification numbers and contact details of on-site staff.

The ML engineer must ensure that sensitive data is masked before sharing the dataset with the data science team for model development, while preserving the meaningful structure and order of the data. 


Which solution will meet these requirements? 


Correct answer
Use Amazon Comprehend with Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler to mask sensitive data fields. Use Data Wrangler to prepare and transform the dataset
Explanation
Correct. Amazon Comprehend is a natural language processing (NLP) service that can detect and locate PII entity types — such as names, identification numbers, phone numbers, and contact details — within text data. When integrated with Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler, it enables automated PII redaction as a built-in transformation step during data preparation, replacing sensitive values with placeholders (e.g., [REDACTED]) rather than deleting the fields entirely. This approach preserves the structural integrity, column ordering, and temporal sequence of features such as inspection dates and billing amounts, which are critical for the downstream ML model. Keyword tip: When the requirement is to mask (not delete) PII while keeping tabular data structure intact for ML pipelines, the Amazon Comprehend + SageMaker Data Wrangler integration is the canonical AWS solution for MLA-C01 scenarios.
Use Amazon Inspector to monitor and alert on sensitive data before sharing the dataset with the team
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Inspector is a security vulnerability assessment service that evaluates AWS compute resources — such as Amazon EC2 instances and container images — for software vulnerabilities and unintended network exposure; it produces prioritized security findings at the infrastructure layer. It has no capability to detect, redact, or mask PII fields within structured tabular datasets, making it wholly unsuitable for this data preparation requirement. Keyword tip: When a question describes scanning AWS workloads or container images for CVEs or compliance deviations, Amazon Inspector is the signal — not when the requirement involves masking PII within ML training datasets.
Store the dataset in an Amazon S3 bucket with encryption enabled and share the data using Amazon Athena. Connect Athena to Amazon SageMaker to run the ML model
Explanation
Incorrect. Enabling server-side encryption on an Amazon S3 bucket protects data at rest from unauthorized physical or storage-layer access, but it does not redact or mask PII fields within the data itself — the data science team would still access the raw sensitive values once the data is read. Connecting Athena to SageMaker addresses query access patterns, not PII governance at the content level. Keyword tip: S3 encryption and access control address the storage confidentiality layer, not the data content masking layer — distinguishing these two separate concerns is a recurring MLA-C01 data governance concept.
Use AWS Glue to remove sensitive data fields entirely before sharing the dataset
Explanation
Incorrect. While AWS Glue supports robust ETL and data transformation workflows, entirely removing sensitive data fields does not satisfy the stated requirement to preserve the meaningful structure and order of the data. Deleting columns such as employee ID or contact fields eliminates contextual relational structure and may discard temporal ordering signals — such as the sequence of service billing amounts — that are relevant to model training. Keyword tip: When a question explicitly requires preserving data structure while protecting sensitive values, any deletion-based approach is disqualified — the correct signal is a masking or redaction solution that replaces rather than removes.
Overall explanation

What this question tests

This question tests the ML engineer’s understanding of PII redaction strategies during data preparation for machine learning workflows on AWS.

It specifically validates the distinction between:

    Masking sensitive data

    Encrypting sensitive data

    Monitoring infrastructure vulnerabilities

    Deleting sensitive fields entirely

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning domain.

The concepts at a glance

Option 1 — Amazon Comprehend with SageMaker Data Wrangler

Use Amazon Comprehend with Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler to mask sensitive data fields. Use Data Wrangler to prepare and transform the dataset.

Amazon Comprehend is a natural language processing service that can detect and locate PII entity types within text data.

Examples of PII include:

PII typeExampleNamePerson namePhone numberContact numberEmail addressContact detailIdentification numberGovernment or customer IDAddressPhysical location information

When integrated with Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler, Amazon Comprehend can help identify and mask sensitive fields while preserving the structure and usability of the dataset for ML workflows.

This is the best fit when the requirement is to mask PII, not delete the data entirely.

Option 2 — Amazon Inspector

Use Amazon Inspector to monitor and alert on sensitive data before sharing the dataset with the team.

Amazon Inspector is a security vulnerability assessment service.

It evaluates AWS compute resources, such as Amazon EC2 instances, container images, and workloads, for issues like:

Amazon Inspector focusExampleSoftware vulnerabilitiesCVEs in packagesNetwork exposureUnintended reachable portsContainer image risksVulnerable dependenciesCompute security findingsPrioritized remediation items

Amazon Inspector is not designed to redact, mask, or transform PII inside datasets.

Therefore, it does not satisfy the requirement for preparing a privacy-preserving ML dataset.

Option 3 — S3 encryption with Athena and SageMaker

Store the dataset in an Amazon S3 bucket with encryption enabled and share the data using Amazon Athena. Connect Athena to Amazon SageMaker to run the ML model.

Enabling encryption on Amazon S3 protects data at rest.

However, encryption does not remove, redact, or mask PII from the dataset itself.

That means the data science team would still be able to query and access the original sensitive values after decryption through authorized services.

ControlWhat it protectsS3 encryptionData at restIAM policiesAccess permissionsAthenaQuery accessSageMakerML workflow executionPII maskingSensitive field exposure

S3 encryption is important for security, but it is not a PII redaction strategy.

Option 4 — AWS Glue to remove sensitive fields entirely

Use AWS Glue to remove sensitive data fields entirely before sharing the dataset.

AWS Glue supports robust ETL workflows and can transform, filter, or remove columns from datasets.

However, entirely removing sensitive fields may not satisfy the requirement when the dataset must preserve its meaningful structure and order for ML preparation.

For example, if the requirement is to keep the data usable while hiding sensitive values, masking is preferred over deletion.

ApproachResultRemove fieldData is deletedMask fieldData structure is preservedEncrypt fieldData remains protected at rest or in transit, but may still be readable by authorized usersDetect and redact PIISensitive values are replaced or hidden

Because the scenario requires masking sensitive data while preserving dataset usability, AWS Glue deletion is not the best answer.

Why Amazon Comprehend with SageMaker Data Wrangler fits this scenario

Use Amazon Comprehend with Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler to mask sensitive data fields. Use Data Wrangler to prepare and transform the dataset.

This option fits because Amazon Comprehend can detect and locate PII entities such as names, identification numbers, phone numbers, addresses, and contact details within text data.

SageMaker Data Wrangler can then be used to prepare, transform, and process the dataset before it is used in an ML pipeline.

This combination allows the ML team to preserve the dataset’s structure while reducing exposure of sensitive information.

In practical terms:

RequirementCorrect AWS capabilityDetect PII in textAmazon ComprehendMask or redact PIIAmazon Comprehend integrationPrepare ML datasetSageMaker Data WranglerPreserve tabular structureMasking instead of deletionUse data safely in ML pipelineRedacted dataset preparation

This is why Amazon Comprehend + SageMaker Data Wrangler is the strongest fit.

Exam tip

When the requirement is to mask PII, not delete it, while keeping the tabular data structure intact for ML pipelines, the canonical AWS solution is:

    Amazon Comprehend + Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler

Do not confuse:

RequirementBest choiceMask PII in text data for ML preparationAmazon Comprehend with SageMaker Data WranglerEncrypt stored dataAmazon S3 encryptionDetect vulnerabilities in compute resourcesAmazon InspectorPerform ETL transformationsAWS GlueDelete sensitive columns entirelyAWS Glue or ETL filteringQuery datasets in S3Amazon Athena

Encryption protects access to stored data, but it does not anonymize the data.

Deletion removes fields, but it may destroy useful structure.

Masking preserves structure while reducing sensitive data exposure.

Domain
Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML)
Question 15Skipped

A logistics and freight company, FreightCore Solutions, uses an AWS Lambda function to monitor shipment delay prediction metrics from an ML model deployed in Amazon SageMaker.

The company wants to be notified via email whenever the prediction model's confidence score drops below a specific threshold, such as 80%. The ML engineer needs to implement an automated solution that triggers email notifications whenever the confidence score breaches the threshold. 

Which of the following options should be combined to develop a solution that addresses these requirements? (Select TWO.) 
Correct selection
Log the metrics from the Lambda function to Amazon CloudWatch
Explanation
Correct. AWS Lambda integrates natively with Amazon CloudWatch, enabling you to publish custom metrics from within your Lambda function using the CloudWatch PutMetricData API via the AWS SDK. The confidence score returned by the SageMaker endpoint can be emitted as a custom CloudWatch metric namespace, making it available for dashboards, anomaly detection, and most importantly, as the data source for a CloudWatch Alarm. Ensure the Lambda execution role includes the cloudwatch:PutMetricData IAM permission to authorize metric publishing. Keyword tip: Custom metric publishing from Lambda always targets Amazon CloudWatch — this is the foundational step for any metric-based alerting pipeline in an MLOps monitoring architecture.
Correct selection
Set up a CloudWatch alarm to send the email message
Explanation

Correct. Amazon CloudWatch Alarms natively monitor metrics and automate notifications via Amazon SNS. To implement threshold-based email alerts, you create a CloudWatch Alarm that monitors the custom metric (e.g., the shipment delay prediction model's confidence score), define the breach condition (e.g., value below 80%), and associate an Amazon SNS topic with subscribed email addresses as the alarm action. When the alarm state transitions from OK to ALARM, SNS automatically delivers the email notification to all subscribers. Keyword tip: For metric threshold plus email notification scenarios on the MLA-C01 exam, the canonical pattern is always CloudWatch Alarm → SNS topic → email subscription.
Set up a CloudFormation change set to send the email message
Explanation
Incorrect. A CloudFormation change set is a preview mechanism that shows how proposed modifications to a CloudFormation stack will affect currently running resources before those changes are applied. It is entirely scoped to infrastructure lifecycle management and has no ability to evaluate metric thresholds or trigger email notifications. Keyword tip: Email alerts driven by metric conditions require a CloudWatch Alarm integrated with Amazon SNS — not CloudFormation change sets, which exist solely within the infrastructure provisioning workflow.
Log the metrics from the Lambda function to AWS CloudFormation
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS CloudFormation is an infrastructure-as-code (IaC) service used to define, provision, and manage AWS resources via declarative templates. It has no capability to ingest, store, or monitor runtime metrics emitted by a Lambda function or a SageMaker model endpoint. Keyword tip: When CloudFormation appears in a monitoring or alerting context, treat it as a distractor — CloudFormation manages resource provisioning, not operational observability or metric-driven notifications.
Log the metrics from the Lambda function to AWS CloudTrail
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS CloudTrail is designed for auditing API calls and tracking user activity and resource-level changes across an AWS account, not for logging or monitoring custom application metrics such as model confidence scores. It cannot publish or store time-series metric data that a threshold-based alarm could evaluate. Keyword tip: When a scenario involves compliance, governance, or API-call auditing, think CloudTrail — but for custom application-level metric collection and alerting in an MLOps pipeline, CloudTrail is always a distractor.
Overall explanation

What this question tests

This question tests the MLA-C01 competency of designing automated monitoring and alerting pipelines for deployed ML models on AWS.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance

Set up a CloudWatch alarm to send the email message

Amazon CloudWatch Alarms natively monitor metrics and automate notifications through Amazon SNS.

This is the correct mechanism when the requirement is to send an email notification after a metric breaches a defined threshold.

Log the metrics from the Lambda function to Amazon CloudWatch

AWS Lambda integrates natively with Amazon CloudWatch, enabling you to publish custom metrics from within the Lambda function by using the CloudWatch PutMetricData API through the AWS SDK.

This is the right approach when the application needs to emit custom ML metrics, such as a model confidence score, for monitoring and alerting.

Log the metrics from the Lambda function to AWS CloudTrail

AWS CloudTrail is designed for auditing API calls and tracking user activity and resource-level changes across an AWS account.

It is not designed for logging or monitoring custom application metrics such as model confidence scores.

Log the metrics from the Lambda function to AWS CloudFormation

AWS CloudFormation is an infrastructure-as-code service used to define, provision, and manage AWS resources through declarative templates.

It is not a monitoring service and cannot be used as a destination for custom runtime metrics from a Lambda function.

Set up a CloudFormation change set to send the email message

A CloudFormation change set is a preview mechanism that shows how proposed modifications to a CloudFormation stack will affect existing resources before the changes are applied.

It is not an alerting mechanism and does not send metric-based email notifications.

Why “Set up a CloudWatch alarm to send the email message” and “Log the metrics from the Lambda function to Amazon CloudWatch” fit this scenario

To implement threshold-based email alerts, you create a CloudWatch Alarm that monitors the custom metric, such as the shipment delay prediction model's confidence score.

You then define the breach condition, such as a confidence score below 80%, and associate the alarm with an Amazon SNS topic that has subscribed email recipients.

The confidence score returned by the SageMaker endpoint can be emitted as a custom CloudWatch metric namespace by the Lambda function.

Once the metric is available in CloudWatch, it can be used for dashboards, anomaly detection, and most importantly, as the data source for a CloudWatch Alarm.

This creates the correct event flow:

Lambda function publishes custom metric → CloudWatch stores metric → CloudWatch Alarm evaluates threshold → SNS sends email notification

Exam tip

For metric-threshold plus email-notification scenarios on the MLA-C01 exam, the canonical pattern is:

CloudWatch custom metric → CloudWatch Alarm → SNS topic → email subscription

Remember:

Custom application metric: Publish to Amazon CloudWatch using PutMetricData.

Threshold-based alert: Use a CloudWatch Alarm.

Email notification: Use Amazon SNS.

API auditing: Use AWS CloudTrail.

Infrastructure provisioning: Use AWS CloudFormation.

Question 16Skipped

A consumer electronics manufacturer, NovaTech Devices, has trained an ML model in Amazon SageMaker to predict product return rates based on real-time customer usage telemetry. The company needs to deploy the model in a production environment to meet the following requirements:

  - Ensure high availability to provide predictions without interruptions. - Achieve low latency for real-time predictions on return risk assessments.
- Process input data sizes ranging between 1 KB and 5 MB.
- Handle unpredictable surges in requests during peak sales and post-holiday return periods.
- Scale dynamically to match fluctuating demand throughout the day.
Which of the following represents the best solution for the given scenario?
Deploy the model using Amazon SageMaker asynchronous inference to handle unpredictable surges and process requests efficiently to meet strict low-latency requirements.
Explanation

Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference queues incoming requests and processes them asynchronously, making it well-suited for large payloads up to 1 GB and long-running inference jobs where higher latency is acceptable. It does not return responses synchronously, which makes it fundamentally incompatible with NovaTech Devices' strict real-time, low-latency requirement for per-request return risk assessments. Keyword tip: Asynchronous Inference is the right fit for near-real-time use cases with relaxed latency constraints and very large payloads — it becomes a distractor the moment the scenario mandates synchronous, millisecond-level response times.
Correct answer
Deploy the model using Amazon SageMaker real-time inference with an auto-scaling endpoint to manage bursts in traffic and provide high availability.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker real-time inference provides dedicated, persistent endpoints that deliver millisecond-level latency predictions, support payload sizes up to 6 MB (accommodating NovaTech Devices' 1 KB–5 MB requirement), and integrate natively with Application Auto Scaling to handle unpredictable surges during peak return periods. This combination of low latency, continuous high availability, and dynamic scaling satisfies every constraint stated in the scenario, making it the optimal deployment choice. Keyword tip: whenever a scenario requires millisecond latency, payload sizes up to 6 MB, uninterrupted availability, and demand-driven auto-scaling, Amazon SageMaker real-time inference with an auto-scaling endpoint is the correct answer.
Deploy the model using Amazon SageMaker batch transform to process predictions in batches during peak hours to optimize resource utilization.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform is designed to run inference on large, static datasets in an offline, non-interactive manner. It does not support real-time, on-demand predictions and cannot fulfill the low-latency requirement for return risk assessments during peak sales periods. Batch Transform jobs are initiated on a schedule or trigger and return results after the full batch is processed, making them incompatible with synchronous, per-request prediction needs. Keyword tip: Batch Transform is a distractor whenever the scenario requires real-time or near-real-time inference — it is intended exclusively for bulk, offline scoring of pre-collected datasets.
Use Amazon SageMaker serverless inference to automatically scale inferences based on demand, eliminating the need for endpoint management.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Serverless Inference is designed for workloads with idle periods between traffic spurts and can tolerate cold starts during scale-up. While it eliminates infrastructure management overhead, it is not suitable for strict real-time, low-latency requirements because cold start latency can be unpredictable and disruptive for time-sensitive return risk predictions. NovaTech Devices requires continuous, low-latency availability — a guarantee that Serverless Inference cannot provide when cold starts are possible. Keyword tip: when a scenario demands consistently low latency and continuous availability with no tolerance for cold starts, Serverless Inference is a distractor — it is only appropriate when intermittent idle periods are acceptable.
Overall explanation

What this question tests

This question tests the ability to select the appropriate Amazon SageMaker inference mode based on a combination of latency, availability, payload size, and scalability requirements , a core MLOps deployment decision pattern in the MLA-C01 exam. It is tied to the Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows domain. 

The concepts at a glance 

- Deploy the model using Amazon SageMaker real-time inference with an auto-scaling endpoint to manage bursts in traffic and provide high availability: Amazon SageMaker real-time inference provides dedicated, persistent endpoints that deliver millisecond-level latency predictions, support payload sizes up to 6 MB and integrate natively with Application Auto Scaling

- Use Amazon SageMaker serverless inference to automatically scale inferences based on demand, eliminating the need for endpoint management: Amazon SageMaker Serverless Inference is designed for workloads with idle periods between traffic spurts and can tolerate cold starts during scale-up.

- Deploy the model using Amazon SageMaker batch transform to process predictions in batches during peak hours to optimize resource utilization: Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform is designed to run inference on large, static datasets in an offline, non-interactive manner.

- Deploy the model using Amazon SageMaker asynchronous inference to handle unpredictable surges and process requests efficiently to meet strict low-latency requirements: Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference queues incoming requests and processes them asynchronously, making it well-suited for large payloads up to 1 GB and long-running inference jobs where higher latency is acceptable. 
Why Deploy the model using Amazon SageMaker real-time inference with an auto-scaling endpoint to manage bursts in traffic and provide high availability fits this scenario Amazon SageMaker real-time inference provides dedicated, persistent endpoints that deliver millisecond-level latency predictions, support payload sizes up to 6 MB (accommodating NovaTech Devices' 1 KB-5 MB requirement), and integrate natively with Application Auto Scaling to handle unpredictable surges during peak return periods.
This combination of low latency, continuous high availability, and dynamic scaling satisfies every constraint stated in the scenario, making it the optimal deployment choice.

Exam tip whenever a scenario requires millisecond latency, payload sizes up to 6 MB, uninterrupted availability, and demand-driven auto-scaling, Amazon SageMaker real-time inference with an auto-scaling endpoint is the correct answer.

Question 17Skipped

A data science team at OceanRoute Logistics, a maritime shipping company, is developing a machine learning model to predict vessel engine failure.

The dataset being used contains hundreds of features, but the team suspects that not all of these features are equally important for the model's accuracy.

To improve model performance and reduce its complexity, the team wants to focus on identifying only the most relevant features that contribute significantly to minimizing the model's error rate.


Which feature engineering process should the team apply to select a subset of features that are the most relevant towards minimizing the error rate of the trained model? 
Feature extraction
Explanation

Incorrect. Feature extraction reduces the dimensionality of input data by constructing new composite representations using techniques such as Principal Component Analysis (PCA), Independent Component Analysis (ICA), and Linear Discriminant Analysis (LDA). Although the number of features processed decreases, the approach synthesizes entirely new components from the original feature space rather than retaining a subset of existing features ranked by their contribution to minimizing error. Keyword tip: when you see 'dimensionality reduction,' 'PCA,' 'ICA,' or 'composite components derived from original features,' think feature extraction, not feature selection.
Correct answer
Feature selection
Explanation
Correct. Feature selection is the process of identifying and retaining only the subset of existing features that are most relevant to the prediction task and that contribute most to minimizing the trained model's error rate. Techniques such as feature importance scores from tree-based models, mutual information ranking, and correlation matrix filtering are commonly used to evaluate and remove low-value features. This directly addresses OceanRoute Logistics' goal of reducing model complexity while preserving predictive accuracy for vessel engine failure prediction. Keyword tip: when you see 'subset of existing features,' 'error rate minimization,' or 'feature importance scores used to prune irrelevant variables,' think feature selection.

Feature transformation
Explanation

Incorrect. Feature transformation involves replacing missing or invalid values and applying mathematical or structural changes to existing features. Techniques include forming Cartesian products of features, applying non-linear transformations such as binning numeric variables into categories, and creating domain-specific representations. It does not address choosing a subset of features based on their relevance to minimizing model error rate. Keyword tip: when you see 'imputation,' 'non-linear transformation,' or 'binning numeric variables,' think feature transformation rather than any selection process.
Feature creation
Explanation

Incorrect. Feature creation refers to generating new features from existing raw data to improve predictive power. Common examples include one-hot encoding, discretization binning, feature splitting, and engineered calculated fields such as fuel-consumption-per-nautical-mile ratios. This process enriches the feature space rather than pruning it to retain only the most relevant existing features for error minimization. Keyword tip: when you see 'one-hot encoding,' 'new derived features,' or 'calculated fields generated from raw data,' think feature creation, not feature selection.
Overall explanation

What this question tests
This question tests the candidate's understanding of the distinct stages of feature engineering in the ML data preparation lifecycle. It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain. The concepts at a glance - Feature selection: Feature selection is the process of identifying and retaining only the subset of existing features that are most relevant to the prediction task and that contribute most to minimizing the trained model's error rate.

- Feature transformation: Feature transformation involves replacing missing or invalid values and applying mathematical or structural changes to existing features.
- Feature extraction: Feature extraction reduces the dimensionality of input data by constructing new composite representations using techniques such as Principal Component Analysis (PCA), Independent Component Analysis (ICA), and Linear Discriminant Analysis (LDA).
- Feature creation: Feature creation refers to generating new features from existing raw data to improve predictive power. Why Feature selection fits this scenario This directly addresses OceanRoute Logistics' goal of reducing model complexity while preserving predictive accuracy for vessel engine failure prediction. Exam tip when you see 'subset of existing features,' 'error rate minimization,' or 'feature importance scores used to prune irrelevant variables,' think feature selection. Question 18Skipped

An agricultural technology company, AgroVision Systems, uses an ML model to generate text descriptions for crop field images uploaded by farmers through a mobile application. Each uploaded image can be as large as 40 MB in size. To cost-effectively handle inference requests for these uploads, the images are saved in an Amazon S3 bucket.


A data engineer needs to implement a scalable processing solution that can adapt to fluctuations in demand while minimizing operational overhead. 

What is the best solution to meet these requirements?



Correct answer
Create an Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference endpoint and a scaling policy. Upon each image upload, trigger an AWS Lambda function to make an inference request.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference is the optimal choice for this scenario. It queues incoming requests and processes them asynchronously, supporting large payload sizes up to 1 GB and processing times up to one hour — both far exceeding the 40 MB image size requirement. It also supports autoscaling the instance count to zero when there are no requests to process, reducing costs during idle periods. Triggering an AWS Lambda function on each S3 image upload provides an event-driven, scalable, and low-overhead invocation mechanism that aligns with the demand-fluctuation requirement. Keyword tip: Large payload exceeding 6 MB, near-real-time processing, cost optimization via scale-to-zero, and event-driven S3 uploads are the canonical signals pointing to Asynchronous Inference combined with AWS Lambda.
Utilize Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform for inference to process the images stored in the Amazon S3 bucket in bulk.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform is a serverless, scalable, and cost-effective solution for offline predictions on large batches of data available upfront. It natively processes data in structured formats such as CSV or JSON. Binary image file formats (e.g., JPEG, PNG) are not natively supported by Batch Transform, making this option unsuitable for processing raw image files stored in Amazon S3 on an event-driven, per-upload basis. Keyword tip: Batch Transform suits scheduled, bulk, structured-data workloads — not event-driven binary image processing triggered by individual S3 uploads.
Create an Amazon SageMaker Real-Time Inference endpoint. Upon each image upload, trigger an AWS Lambda function to make an inference request.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Real-Time Inference is suitable for workloads requiring low-millisecond latency responses. However, it enforces a maximum payload size of only 6 MB and a maximum processing time of 60 seconds. Because each crop field image can be up to 40 MB, this option fails both the payload size constraint and the cost-efficiency requirement, since real-time endpoints cannot scale to zero during idle periods. Keyword tip: Real-Time Inference has a hard 6 MB payload limit and a 60-second timeout — both are disqualifying constraints whenever large binary assets or cost-optimized idle scaling are required.
Create an Amazon SageMaker Serverless Inference endpoint and a scaling policy. Run a script to make an inference request for each image.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Serverless Inference is designed for workloads with idle periods between traffic bursts and can tolerate cold starts. However, the maximum request and response payload size for Serverless Inference is 4 MB, which makes it wholly unsuitable for field images up to 40 MB in size. Additionally, running a script to sequentially invoke individual inference requests does not constitute a scalable, event-driven architecture appropriate for unpredictable upload volumes. Keyword tip: When a question mentions payload limits alongside Serverless Inference, always verify the hard 4 MB payload constraint as an automatic disqualifier.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the candidate's ability to select the appropriate Amazon SageMaker inference endpoint type based on payload size constraints, processing model, and cost optimization requirements.

It is tied to the Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference: The best choice for large payloads, longer processing times, and near-real-time asynchronous processing.

    Amazon SageMaker Serverless Inference: Best for intermittent workloads with smaller payloads and acceptable cold starts.

    Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform: Best for offline bulk inference when all input data is available upfront.

    Amazon SageMaker Real-Time Inference: Best for synchronous, low-latency inference with persistent endpoint capacity.

Why Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference fits this scenario.

Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference is the optimal choice because it supports large payloads and asynchronous processing.

It supports payload sizes up to 1 GB and processing times up to one hour, which comfortably exceeds the 40 MB image size requirement.

A common architecture is:

Amazon S3 image upload → AWS Lambda trigger → SageMaker Asynchronous Inference request → asynchronous processing → output stored in Amazon S3.

This approach also supports cost optimization because Asynchronous Inference endpoints can scale down to zero instances when there are no requests to process.

That makes it a strong fit for event-driven workloads where image uploads occur intermittently and immediate millisecond-level responses are not required.

Exam tip.

Large payloads, near-real-time processing, cost optimization through scale-to-zero, and event-driven Amazon S3 uploads are the canonical signals for Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference combined with AWS Lambda.

Remember:

    Large payloads up to 1 GB: Use Asynchronous Inference.

    Small intermittent payloads with cold-start tolerance: Use Serverless Inference.

    Offline bulk prediction on data available upfront: Use Batch Transform.

    Low-latency synchronous prediction: Use Real-Time Inference.

    Event-driven inference after S3 upload: Use S3 event notification with AWS Lambda.

Official AWS references.

    Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference.

    Amazon SageMaker Hosting FAQs.

Key exam terms: Asynchronous Inference, payload size limit, scale-to-zero, AWS Lambda, Amazon S3 event trigger, Serverless Inference 4 MB constraint, Real-Time Inference 6 MB limit, operational overhead.

Question 19Skipped

A data science team at UrbanIQ Systems, a smart city government agency, is evaluating foundational machine learning algorithms for a new citizen service segmentation and classification project.

How would you correctly differentiate between the K-Means and K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN) algorithms in the context of machine learning?  

K-Means is primarily used for regression tasks, while KNN is used for reducing the dimensionality of data
Explanation
Incorrect. K-Means is not used for regression tasks; it is an unsupervised clustering algorithm that partitions data into k distinct groups by iteratively minimizing intra-cluster variance. KNN is not a dimensionality reduction technique — it is a supervised algorithm used for classification and regression based on proximity to labeled neighbors in the feature space. Dimensionality reduction is addressed by dedicated techniques such as PCA or t-SNE, neither of which is K-Means or KNN. Keyword tip: when a distractor pairs 'K-Means with regression' or 'KNN with dimensionality reduction,' it is incorrect by definition — associate dimensionality reduction with PCA or t-SNE, not with these two algorithms.
K-Means is a supervised learning algorithm used for classification, while KNN is an unsupervised learning algorithm used for clustering
Explanation
Incorrect. This option reverses the learning paradigms of both algorithms. K-Means is unsupervised — it requires no labeled data and discovers inherent groupings in a dataset. KNN is supervised — it relies entirely on labeled training examples to assign a class or predict a value for new data points. Misattributing supervision to K-Means and unsupervised learning to KNN is a common distractor pattern on the MLA-C01 exam. Keyword tip: reversed learning paradigm distractors are frequent — always determine whether labeled data is required to correctly distinguish supervised from unsupervised algorithms.
K-Means requires labeled data to form clusters, whereas KNN does not use labeled data for making predictions
Explanation
Incorrect. This option inverts the label requirements of both algorithms. K-Means does not require labeled data — it discovers latent structure in unlabeled data by grouping points based on feature similarity and centroid distance. KNN, conversely, explicitly requires a labeled training set because it assigns predictions based on the known classes of the k nearest neighboring points. Keyword tip: any option stating that K-Means requires labels is incorrect by definition — the absence of labeled data is the defining characteristic of unsupervised algorithms such as K-Means.
Correct answer
K-Means is an unsupervised learning algorithm used for clustering data points into groups, while KNN is a supervised learning algorithm used for classifying data points based on their proximity to labeled examples
Explanation
Correct. K-Means is an unsupervised learning algorithm that partitions a dataset into k clusters by iteratively reassigning data points to the nearest centroid and recalculating centroids until convergence — it requires no labeled data. KNN is a supervised, instance-based learning algorithm that classifies a new data point by identifying its k nearest labeled neighbors in the training set and applying a majority vote (for classification) or weighted average (for regression). Amazon SageMaker provides optimized built-in implementations of both: the K-Means algorithm for unsupervised clustering and the KNN algorithm for supervised classification and regression at scale. Keyword tip: 'unsupervised + centroid-based grouping = K-Means' and 'supervised + proximity to labeled neighbors = KNN' is the core distinction tested on MLA-C01.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the fundamental distinction between unsupervised and supervised machine learning paradigms.

It specifically contrasts K-Means clustering with K-Nearest Neighbors classification.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    K-Means is an unsupervised learning algorithm used for clustering data points into groups, while KNN is a supervised learning algorithm used for classifying data points based on their proximity to labeled examples: K-Means partitions a dataset into k clusters by iteratively assigning data points to the nearest centroid and recalculating centroids until convergence. It does not require labeled data. K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN) uses labeled examples to classify new data points based on proximity to their nearest neighbors.

    K-Means is primarily used for regression tasks, while KNN is used for reducing the dimensionality of data: K-Means is not used for regression tasks. It is an unsupervised clustering algorithm. KNN is not a dimensionality reduction technique; it is commonly used for classification and regression based on nearby labeled examples.

    K-Means is a supervised learning algorithm used for classification, while KNN is an unsupervised learning algorithm used for clustering: This option reverses the learning paradigms of both algorithms. K-Means is unsupervised, while KNN is supervised.

    K-Means requires labeled data to form clusters, whereas KNN does not use labeled data for making predictions: This option inverts the label requirements. K-Means does not require labels, while KNN depends on labeled training examples to classify or predict new data points.

Why K-Means as unsupervised clustering and KNN as supervised classification fit this scenario.

K-Means is an unsupervised learning algorithm.

It groups data points into clusters based on similarity, without using labeled target values.

The algorithm works by selecting k centroids, assigning each data point to the nearest centroid, recalculating the centroids, and repeating this process until the clusters stabilize.

K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN) is a supervised learning algorithm.

It uses labeled training examples to classify or predict the value of a new data point.

For classification, KNN looks at the nearest labeled examples and assigns the class most common among those neighbors.

This is the core distinction:

    K-Means: Unsupervised clustering without labels.

    KNN: Supervised prediction using labeled neighbors.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Unsupervised + centroid-based grouping = K-Means.

Supervised + proximity to labeled neighbors = KNN.

Remember:

    K-Means: Clustering.

    K-Means: No labeled data required.

    KNN: Classification or regression.

    KNN: Labeled data required.

    Centroids: K-Means.

    Nearest labeled examples: KNN.

Official AWS references.

    K-Means clustering with Amazon SageMaker.

    K-Nearest Neighbors in Amazon SageMaker.

Key exam terms: K-Means, K-Nearest Neighbors, unsupervised learning, supervised learning, clustering, classification, labeled data, Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithms.

Question 20Skipped

A machine learning (ML) engineer at PharmaGen Research has developed a model using Amazon SageMaker and deployed it into production to predict compound efficacy outcomes.

The engineer wants to track the performance of the model in real time, including metrics such as prediction accuracy and model drift, and ensure that the model maintains high quality over time as new experimental data enters the pipeline.

Which of the following AWS services is the most suitable for monitoring the model's performance for this scenario?
AWS Glue
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Glue is a serverless data integration service designed for extracting, transforming, and loading (ETL) data. It is used to prepare and transform data for analytics and machine learning workflows, not to monitor deployed model performance in production. It lacks capabilities for tracking prediction accuracy, detecting data drift, or triggering alerts based on model quality degradation over time. Keyword tip: when you see 'ETL', 'data cataloging', or 'data preparation' in an ML pipeline context, AWS Glue is the likely correct answer — not a post-deployment monitoring solution.
Correct answer
Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor is purpose-built for continuously monitoring the quality of machine learning models deployed in production on Amazon SageMaker. It supports four monitoring types: (1) Data quality — detects drift in the statistical properties of input data relative to a baseline; (2) Model quality — tracks drift in model performance metrics such as accuracy against ground truth labels; (3) Bias drift — monitors changes in prediction bias over time; and (4) Feature attribution drift — monitors shifts in SHAP-based feature importance. It integrates natively with Amazon CloudWatch to trigger automated alerts when model quality deviates from predefined thresholds, making it the correct fit for PharmaGen Research's real-time monitoring requirements. Keyword tip: any scenario mentioning 'real-time model monitoring', 'prediction accuracy drift', 'data drift', 'bias monitoring', or 'feature attribution drift' for a deployed SageMaker endpoint points directly to Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor.
Amazon SageMaker Autopilot
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Autopilot automates the end-to-end model building process, including data preprocessing, algorithm selection, hyperparameter tuning, and model training. Its scope is limited to the model development and training phase. It does not provide any capabilities to monitor deployed models in production for performance degradation, data drift, bias drift, or feature attribution drift. Keyword tip: 'AutoML', 'automated model building', or 'automatic algorithm selection' point to SageMaker Autopilot; post-deployment quality and drift monitoring of a live endpoint points to SageMaker Model Monitor.
Amazon CloudWatch
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon CloudWatch is a general-purpose monitoring and observability service for AWS infrastructure and applications. While it can collect system-level metrics such as CPU utilization, memory, and endpoint invocation latency from SageMaker endpoints, it does not natively support ML-specific monitoring such as data quality drift detection, model quality drift, bias drift, or feature attribution drift. In the context of SageMaker Model Monitor, CloudWatch is used as a downstream recipient of monitoring outputs and alert notifications — not as the primary ML model quality monitoring solution. Keyword tip: CloudWatch handles infrastructure-level and operational metrics; for ML-specific model quality and drift monitoring on a deployed SageMaker endpoint, Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor is the correct choice.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of the appropriate AWS service for post-deployment ML model quality monitoring.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor: Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor is purpose-built for continuously monitoring the quality of machine learning models deployed in production on Amazon SageMaker. It can monitor data quality, model quality, bias drift, and feature attribution drift.

    AWS Glue: AWS Glue is a serverless data integration service designed for extracting, transforming, and loading data. It is used for ETL and data preparation, not for monitoring deployed model quality.

    Amazon CloudWatch: Amazon CloudWatch is a general-purpose monitoring and observability service for AWS infrastructure and applications. It can collect metrics, logs, and alarms, but it is not the primary ML-specific service for detecting model drift or model quality degradation.

    Amazon SageMaker Autopilot: Amazon SageMaker Autopilot automates the end-to-end model building process, including data preprocessing, algorithm selection, hyperparameter tuning, and model training. It is used during model development, not for post-deployment model monitoring.

Why Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor fits this scenario.

Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor is the correct fit because it is designed to monitor deployed SageMaker models in production.

It helps detect changes in model behavior and data characteristics over time.

It supports monitoring for data quality drift, model quality drift, bias drift, and feature attribution drift.

It also integrates natively with Amazon CloudWatch to trigger automated alerts when model quality deviates from predefined thresholds.

This makes it the correct fit for PharmaGen Research's real-time model monitoring requirements.

Exam tip.

Any scenario mentioning real-time model monitoring, prediction accuracy drift, data drift, bias monitoring, or feature attribution drift for a deployed SageMaker endpoint points directly to Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor.

Remember:

    Post-deployment model quality monitoring: Use Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor.

    Infrastructure and application metrics: Use Amazon CloudWatch.

    ETL and data preparation: Use AWS Glue.

    Automated model building: Use Amazon SageMaker Autopilot.

Official AWS references.

Question 21Skipped

An industrial manufacturing company, PrecisionForge Industries, wants to build an ML solution to predict equipment failure events before they occur. The company's dataset includes thousands of rows with features related to machine sensor readings, maintenance history, and production line interactions. The dataset is stored in CSV format in Amazon S3. 

The ML engineer must select an appropriate built-in Amazon SageMaker algorithm for training the model. The algorithm should handle tabular data and be capable of performing supervised learning for a binary classification problem. 

Which Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithm should the ML engineer use?
Linear Learner
Explanation
Incorrect. Linear Learner is a supervised learning algorithm in Amazon SageMaker that can handle both classification and regression problems and is capable of binary classification in principle. However, it is a linear model and therefore less effective than XGBoost for complex, non-linear tabular datasets such as those combining continuous sensor readings, categorical maintenance records, and production line interaction features, where non-linear feature interactions are common. XGBoost's ensemble tree-based approach captures those non-linear patterns far more effectively. Keyword tip: Linear Learner is best suited for high-dimensional, linearly separable problems — when the scenario involves complex tabular data with non-linear patterns or mixed feature types, XGBoost is the preferred SageMaker built-in algorithm.
Correct answer
XGBoost (eXtreme Gradient Boosting)
Explanation
Correct. XGBoost (eXtreme Gradient Boosting) is a highly efficient, open-source implementation of the gradient boosted trees algorithm and is one of the most widely used Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithms for supervised learning on tabular data. It supports binary classification, multiclass classification, regression, and ranking problems natively, and it reads CSV data directly from Amazon S3 without requiring custom preprocessing. XGBoost is robust against a variety of feature types, non-linear relationships, and distributions, and it offers a rich set of hyperparameters for fine-tuning — making it well suited for complex tabular datasets such as sensor readings combined with maintenance history. Keyword tip: for tabular data + supervised learning + binary or multiclass classification or regression tasks on the MLA-C01 exam, XGBoost is almost always the strongest candidate among SageMaker built-in algorithms.
K-Means
Explanation
Incorrect. K-Means is an unsupervised learning algorithm used for clustering, where it partitions data into discrete groups such that members within a group are as similar as possible while being as different as possible from members of other groups. Because equipment failure prediction is a supervised binary classification task — the label (failure or no failure) is already known in the training data — K-Means is fundamentally unsuitable for this scenario. Keyword tip: any question mentioning unsupervised clustering or grouping without labels points to K-Means; binary classification with labeled data requires a supervised algorithm.
BlazingText
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker BlazingText provides highly optimized implementations of the Word2vec and text classification algorithms. It is specifically designed for natural language processing (NLP) tasks such as sentiment analysis, named entity recognition, and machine translation. BlazingText does not support structured tabular data and cannot perform binary classification on sensor readings or maintenance history records. Keyword tip: whenever you see NLP, word embeddings, or text classification in a scenario, BlazingText is the relevant algorithm — not for tabular or structured data tasks.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the ability to select the most appropriate Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithm for a supervised binary classification task on structured tabular data.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    XGBoost: XGBoost, or eXtreme Gradient Boosting, is a highly efficient, open-source implementation of the gradient boosted trees algorithm. It is one of the most widely used Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithms for supervised learning on tabular data.

    BlazingText: Amazon SageMaker BlazingText provides highly optimized implementations of Word2vec and text classification algorithms. It is mainly associated with natural language processing workloads, not general-purpose structured tabular classification.

    K-Means: K-Means is an unsupervised learning algorithm used for clustering. It partitions data into groups so that members within the same group are as similar as possible and members of different groups are as different as possible.

    Linear Learner: Linear Learner is a supervised learning algorithm in Amazon SageMaker that can handle both classification and regression problems. It is capable of binary classification, but it is usually less flexible than XGBoost for complex tabular datasets with non-linear relationships.

Why XGBoost fits this scenario.

XGBoost is the best fit because it is designed for supervised learning tasks on structured tabular data.

It supports binary classification, multiclass classification, regression, and ranking problems natively.

It can read CSV data directly from Amazon S3, which makes it practical for SageMaker training workflows without requiring a custom algorithm implementation.

XGBoost is robust across a variety of feature types, non-linear relationships, and data distributions.

This makes it well suited for complex tabular datasets, such as sensor readings combined with maintenance history.

It also provides a rich set of hyperparameters for fine-tuning model performance.

Exam tip.

For MLA-C01, the key exam signal is:

Tabular data + supervised learning + binary classification = XGBoost.

Remember:

    Complex structured tabular data: Use XGBoost.

    Binary or multiclass classification on tabular data: XGBoost is usually a strong candidate.

    Text classification or Word2vec: Use BlazingText.

    Unsupervised clustering: Use K-Means.

    Linear classification or regression: Use Linear Learner.

Official AWS references.

    Amazon SageMaker XGBoost algorithm.

    Build, tune, and deploy an end-to-end churn prediction model using Amazon SageMaker Pipelines.

Question 22Skipped

A weather and climate research agency, AtmosPredict Institute, has collected historical data containing information about research stations that required extended technical intervention from its data engineering support team. 

The organization now seeks to build an ML model to forecast whether future research stations will need long-term support. 

What approach should the organization take to build and implement this predictive model? 


Correct answer
Logistic regression
Explanation
Correct. Logistic regression is the appropriate technique for this binary classification problem — predicting whether a research station will or will not require long-term support (a yes/no outcome). It models the probability of a binary dependent variable by analyzing relationships between input features and the target class. In Amazon SageMaker, logistic regression can be implemented via the built-in Linear Learner algorithm configured for binary classification mode. Keyword tip: whenever a scenario describes predicting a categorical or binary outcome (e.g., 'will this station require intervention?', 'will this event occur or not?'), logistic regression or a classification algorithm is the correct choice — not a regression or unsupervised method.
Linear regression
Explanation
Incorrect. Linear regression is a supervised learning technique used to predict a continuous numerical value (e.g., forecasting rainfall amounts or estimating temperature). Since the target variable in this scenario is binary — whether a research station will or will not require long-term support — linear regression is not appropriate. The output of linear regression is unbounded and does not produce a valid probability or discrete class label. Keyword tip: use linear regression when predicting a continuous quantity; use logistic regression or a classification algorithm when predicting a discrete category or binary outcome.
Principal Component Analysis (PCA) Algorithm
Explanation
Incorrect. Principal Component Analysis (PCA) is an unsupervised dimensionality reduction technique used to reduce the number of features in a dataset while retaining as much variance as possible. It does not produce predictions or classify observations into labeled categories. PCA is typically used as a preprocessing step before training a downstream model, not as a standalone predictive model for binary outcomes. Keyword tip: if the scenario mentions reducing dimensionality, compressing features, or managing high-dimensional data without a labeled target variable, PCA is the relevant algorithm — not for supervised binary prediction tasks.
Anomaly Detection
Explanation
Incorrect. Anomaly detection is used to identify rare or unusual data points that deviate significantly from an established normal pattern. It is not designed to predict a binary class label for new records based on historical labeled examples. Anomaly detection is well-suited for use cases such as identifying sensor malfunctions or flagging abnormal atmospheric readings in real time, where the goal is to surface outliers rather than classify future observations into known categories. Keyword tip: when a scenario focuses on detecting unusual behavior or outliers in a data stream rather than forecasting a specific labeled outcome for new instances, anomaly detection is the appropriate approach.
Overall explanation

What this question tests

This question tests the ability to select the correct machine learning algorithm type based on the nature of the prediction target.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance

Option 1 — Logistic regression

Logistic regression is the appropriate technique for this binary classification problem: predicting whether a research station will or will not require long-term support.

This is a yes/no outcome, so the target variable is categorical and binary.

Logistic regression is used when the goal is to estimate the probability of a class, such as:

Input scenarioPredicted outcomeResearch station conditionsRequires long-term supportResearch station conditionsDoes not require long-term support

Because the prediction target is not a continuous number, this is a classification problem, not a regression problem.

Option 2 — Principal Component Analysis, or PCA

Principal Component Analysis, or PCA, is an unsupervised dimensionality reduction technique.

It is used to reduce the number of features in a dataset while retaining as much variance as possible.

PCA is useful when you want to simplify high-dimensional data, remove redundancy, or improve model training efficiency.

However, PCA does not directly predict whether an event will happen or not.

Option 3 — Anomaly detection

Anomaly detection is used to identify rare or unusual data points that deviate significantly from an established normal pattern.

It is appropriate for use cases such as:

Use caseGoalFraud detectionFind unusual transactionsIndustrial monitoringDetect abnormal sensor behaviorSecurity monitoringIdentify suspicious activityEquipment monitoringDetect unexpected failure patterns

This scenario is not asking to identify unusual research stations. It is asking to predict a binary outcome.

Option 4 — Linear regression

Linear regression is a supervised learning technique used to predict a continuous numerical value.

Examples include:

Prediction targetProblem typeForecasting rainfall amountRegressionEstimating temperatureRegressionPredicting energy consumptionRegressionEstimating costRegression

Linear regression is not the best fit here because the target is not a continuous value. The question asks whether the station will require long-term support or not.

Why logistic regression fits this scenario

Logistic regression fits this scenario because the problem is a binary classification problem.

The model is not trying to predict a continuous numerical value. It is trying to predict one of two possible classes:

ClassMeaningYesThe research station will require long-term supportNoThe research station will not require long-term support

This makes logistic regression the appropriate algorithm because it is designed to model the probability of a binary outcome.

For example:

    P(long_term_support_required | station_features)

The model estimates the probability that a research station belongs to the positive class.

Exam tip

Whenever a scenario describes predicting a categorical or binary outcome, such as:

    “Will this station require intervention?”

    “Will this event occur or not?”

    “Is this customer likely to churn?”

    “Is this transaction fraudulent?”

    “Should this item be approved or rejected?”

the correct choice is usually logistic regression or another classification algorithm.

Do not confuse the word “regression” in logistic regression with a regression problem.

Target typeCorrect ML taskYes/no outcomeBinary classificationMultiple categoriesMulticlass classificationContinuous numberRegressionHidden structure or feature reductionUnsupervised learningUnusual pattern detectionAnomaly detection
