Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows
Question 47Skipped

A data science team at a sports analytics company called Apex Performance Analytics is onboarding new ML engineers and needs to establish a foundational understanding of deep learning principles before deploying models on AWS.

As part of the onboarding curriculum, the team lead asks the engineers to identify the correct description of how model training works in deep learning.

Which of the following best describes the model training process in deep learning?



Model training in deep learning involves only the use of support vector machines and decision trees to create predictive models.
Explanation
Incorrect. Support vector machines (SVMs) and decision trees are classical machine learning algorithms, not deep learning architectures. Deep learning specifically refers to the use of multi-layer artificial neural networks (ANNs) that learn hierarchical feature representations. While SVMs and decision trees may appear in ensemble or hybrid pipelines, they represent a distinct ML paradigm and do not describe the deep learning training process. Keyword tip: SVMs and decision trees belong to traditional ML, not deep learning — this is a common distractor on MLA-C01 designed to test whether candidates can distinguish between classical ML and neural network-based paradigms.
Model training in deep learning requires no data; the neural network automatically learns from predefined algorithms without any input.
Explanation
Incorrect. Data is an essential prerequisite for training any deep learning model. Without labeled or unlabeled input data, a neural network cannot perform a forward pass, compute a loss, derive gradients, or update its parameters. The claim that a network 'automatically learns from predefined algorithms without any input' fundamentally misrepresents both the supervised and unsupervised learning paradigms that underpin deep learning. Keyword tip: any option asserting that deep learning requires 'no data' is always incorrect on MLA-C01 — data availability and quality are foundational concerns in the ML Model Development domain.
Model training in deep learning involves manually setting the weights and biases of a neural network based on predefined rules.
Explanation
Incorrect. Weights and biases in a deep neural network are never set manually through predefined rules. They are learned automatically during the training process via optimization algorithms such as gradient descent and backpropagation. Manual weight initialization strategies — such as Xavier or He initialization — are used only as a starting point, not as the final learned configuration. Keyword tip: when an option describes deep learning weights as 'manually set' or 'rule-based,' it is always a distractor; the hallmark of deep learning is automated, data-driven parameter adjustment.
Correct answer
Model training in deep learning involves using large datasets to adjust the weights and biases of a neural network through multiple iterations, using techniques such as gradient descent to minimize the error.
Explanation
Correct. Deep learning model training is an iterative, data-driven optimization process in which large datasets are fed into the neural network. During each forward pass, the network generates predictions, and the loss function quantifies the prediction error. Gradient descent variants — such as SGD, Adam, or RMSProp — then compute the gradient of the loss with respect to each weight and update those weights via backpropagation, repeating this cycle across many epochs until the model converges to satisfactory performance. On AWS, this process is operationalized through Amazon SageMaker training jobs, which support built-in deep learning frameworks such as TensorFlow and PyTorch. Keyword tip: 'iterative,' 'gradient descent,' 'minimize error,' and 'adjust weights and biases using large datasets' are the hallmark phrases of correct deep learning training descriptions on MLA-C01.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests foundational knowledge of the deep learning training paradigm, which underpins the ML Model Development domain of the MLA-C01 exam.

The concepts at a glance.

    Model training in deep learning involves using large datasets to adjust the weights and biases of a neural network through multiple iterations, using techniques such as gradient descent to minimize the error: Deep learning model training is an iterative, data-driven optimization process in which large datasets are fed into a neural network.

    Model training in deep learning involves manually setting the weights and biases of a neural network based on predefined rules: Weights and biases in a deep neural network are not manually set through predefined rules. They are learned automatically during training based on the data and the optimization process.

    Model training in deep learning requires no data; the neural network automatically learns from predefined algorithms without any input: Data is an essential prerequisite for training any deep learning model. Without training data, the model has no examples from which to learn patterns.

    Model training in deep learning involves only the use of support vector machines and decision trees to create predictive models: Support vector machines (SVMs) and decision trees are classical machine learning algorithms, not deep learning architectures.

Why deep learning training with large datasets, gradient descent, and iterative weight updates fits this scenario.

Deep learning model training involves feeding large datasets into a neural network and repeatedly adjusting the model’s weights and biases to reduce prediction error.

The model makes predictions, compares them against the expected output using a loss function, and then updates its internal parameters to reduce that loss.

Gradient descent variants, such as SGD, Adam, or RMSProp, compute the gradient of the loss with respect to each weight.

Through backpropagation, those gradients are used to update the network’s weights and biases.

This cycle repeats across many iterations or epochs until the model converges to satisfactory performance.

Exam tip.

The hallmark phrases for correct deep learning training descriptions on MLA-C01 are:

    Iterative optimization.

    Gradient descent.

    Backpropagation.

    Minimize error.

    Adjust weights and biases using large datasets.

Avoid answer choices that describe manual weight setting, no-data learning, or classical ML algorithms as if they were deep learning training.

Official AWS references.

    What is artificial intelligence?

Key exam terms: deep learning training, gradient descent, backpropagation, weights and biases, iterative optimization, Amazon SageMaker training jobs, loss function, neural network.

Question 48Skipped

A mid-size enterprise software vendor, NexaCore Systems, uses an Amazon Redshift database to store transactional and customer data, including sensitive information such as personally identifiable information (PII).

A data analyst needs access to specific columns of the database to perform customer behavior analysis. The company wants to ensure that sensitive data, such as PII, is not exposed to the analyst while maintaining the integrity of the source data.

The solution must avoid duplicating or transforming the data into a separate dataset.   

What do you recommend to address these requirements with the LEAST implementation effort? 


Create a materialized view in Amazon Redshift that excludes sensitive fields and grant the analyst access to the view
Explanation
Incorrect. While a materialized view can exclude sensitive columns and limit what the analyst sees, materialized views require periodic refreshes to stay current with the underlying data and consume additional storage, which increases both operational complexity and cost. They represent a static snapshot that does not dynamically adapt to evolving access policies or real-time data changes. Furthermore, maintaining a separate materialized view constitutes an indirect form of data duplication, directly violating the stated requirement. Keyword tip: When a question forbids duplicating data and demands the least operational overhead on Amazon Redshift, materialized views are disqualified by their storage consumption and refresh scheduling burden.
Correct answer
Implement Amazon Redshift dynamic data masking policies to obfuscate sensitive columns dynamically while allowing the analyst to access permitted data
Explanation
Correct. Amazon Redshift dynamic data masking (DDM) is the most suitable and least-effort solution for this scenario. DDM applies masking policies at query time, obfuscating sensitive column values for users or roles that lack explicit unmasking permissions, without altering or duplicating the underlying source data in any way. It integrates natively with Redshift's role-based access control, enabling granular, policy-driven column-level protection per user or role with minimal configuration overhead. This directly satisfies all stated requirements: column-level PII protection, no data duplication, no data transformation, and the lowest possible implementation effort. Keyword tip: When a scenario on Amazon Redshift demands column-level PII obfuscation at query time with zero data duplication and least effort, dynamic data masking (DDM) is the canonical answer.
Use Amazon Redshift row-level security to restrict access to sensitive rows while allowing access to all columns
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Redshift row-level security (RLS) controls access at the row level, restricting which rows a user can see based on defined policies. It does not address column-level sensitivity, meaning all columns — including those containing PII — remain fully visible to the analyst for any rows that pass the RLS filter. This approach does not satisfy the requirement of protecting sensitive column data from the analyst. Keyword tip: When a scenario requires protecting specific columns containing PII rather than filtering entire rows, RLS is the wrong tool — look for column-level or masking-based controls instead.
Export the data to Amazon S3, apply anonymization to sensitive fields using AWS Glue, and allow the analyst to query the transformed data
Explanation
Incorrect. Exporting data to Amazon S3 and applying anonymization via AWS Glue introduces significant operational overhead, including ETL pipeline development, job scheduling, and ongoing maintenance of both the export and transformation logic. This approach also creates a duplicate copy of the data in S3, directly violating the explicit requirement to avoid duplicating or transforming the data into a separate dataset. Among all options presented, this represents the highest implementation and operational effort. Keyword tip: An S3 export combined with AWS Glue anonymization always implies data duplication and an ETL pipeline — these are disqualifying signals when a question explicitly forbids separate datasets and demands the least effort.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of Amazon Redshift native data protection features and the ability to select the least-effort, no-duplication solution for column-level PII protection.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Implement Amazon Redshift dynamic data masking policies to obfuscate sensitive columns dynamically while allowing the analyst to access permitted data: Amazon Redshift dynamic data masking (DDM) is the most suitable and least-effort solution for this scenario. It masks sensitive column values at query time without changing or duplicating the underlying data.

    Use Amazon Redshift row-level security to restrict access to sensitive rows while allowing access to all columns: Amazon Redshift row-level security (RLS) controls access at the row level. It restricts which rows a user can see based on defined policies, but it does not protect sensitive columns within rows the user is allowed to access.

    Create a materialized view in Amazon Redshift that excludes sensitive fields and grant the analyst access to the view: A materialized view can exclude sensitive columns and limit what the analyst sees. However, materialized views require refreshes to stay current with the underlying data and consume additional storage, which increases operational complexity and cost.

    Export the data to Amazon S3, apply anonymization to sensitive fields using AWS Glue, and allow the analyst to query the transformed data: Exporting data to Amazon S3 and applying anonymization with AWS Glue introduces significant operational overhead. It requires ETL pipeline development, job scheduling, duplicate storage, and ongoing maintenance.

Why Amazon Redshift dynamic data masking fits this scenario.

Amazon Redshift dynamic data masking (DDM) is the most suitable and least-effort solution for this scenario.

DDM applies masking rules dynamically at query time.

This means sensitive values, such as PII, can be obfuscated for unauthorized users while the original source data remains unchanged.

This directly satisfies all stated requirements:

    Column-level PII protection.

    No data duplication.

    No separate transformed dataset.

    Lowest implementation effort.

It is a better fit than row-level security because the requirement is to protect specific columns, not restrict entire rows.

It is also a better fit than materialized views or AWS Glue anonymization because those options introduce additional data artifacts, storage overhead, and maintenance work.

Exam tip.

When a scenario on Amazon Redshift demands column-level PII obfuscation at query time with zero data duplication and least effort, dynamic data masking (DDM) is the canonical answer.

Remember:

    Column-level masking at query time: Use Amazon Redshift dynamic data masking.

    Row-level access control: Use Amazon Redshift row-level security.

    Precomputed query results with storage overhead: Use materialized views.

    Data anonymization through ETL: Use AWS Glue, but expect duplication and operational overhead.

Official AWS references.

    Amazon Redshift dynamic data masking.

    Amazon Redshift materialized views overview.

Key exam terms: Amazon Redshift, dynamic data masking, DDM, column-level PII protection, row-level security, materialized view, no data duplication, query-time obfuscation, least implementation effort.

Question 49Skipped

You are an AI/ML engineer at a commercial real estate firm, NexusRealty Group, that is rapidly expanding its use of generative AI and machine learning to create personalized property search experiences and automated lease analysis workflows.

The company is exploring AWS services to quickly prototype and deploy both generative AI models and traditional machine learning models with minimal operational overhead.

The team is particularly interested in services that provide pre-built models, templates, and the ability to scale solutions into production seamlessly.

  Given these requirements, which of the following statements BEST highlights the differences between Amazon Bedrock and Amazon SageMaker JumpStart to help your team make an informed decision?
Correct answer
Amazon Bedrock focuses on providing a managed service for deploying pre-trained foundation models from various providers, whereas Amazon SageMaker JumpStart offers a range of pre-built solutions, including models, notebooks, and algorithms for both machine learning and generative AI use cases
Explanation
Correct. Amazon Bedrock is a fully managed service that provides access to high-performing foundation models from leading AI providers — including AI21 Labs, Anthropic, Cohere, Meta, Mistral AI, Stability AI, and Amazon — through a single API, making it the primary AWS offering for generative AI application development with built-in security and responsible AI controls. Amazon SageMaker JumpStart complements this by serving as a comprehensive ML hub that accelerates the entire ML journey, providing pre-built model solutions, Jupyter notebooks, algorithms, and managed infrastructure for both traditional ML and generative AI tasks. For NexusRealty Group, Bedrock would support document summarization or conversational search via foundation models, while JumpStart could accelerate property-price prediction models using pre-built algorithm templates. Keyword tip: 'managed service for foundation models from various providers via a single API' maps to Amazon Bedrock; 'pre-built solutions including notebooks and algorithms covering ML and generative AI' maps to Amazon SageMaker JumpStart.
Amazon Bedrock provides a simplified interface for training and tuning models from scratch, while Amazon SageMaker JumpStart is primarily for deploying third-party models with limited customization
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Bedrock does not provide an interface for training models from scratch; its core value proposition is the ability to leverage pre-trained foundation models through an API without managing any underlying infrastructure. Additionally, describing Amazon SageMaker JumpStart as 'primarily for deploying third-party models with limited customization' is inaccurate — JumpStart supports extensive fine-tuning, full training infrastructure, and broad ML solution templates that span a wide variety of domains and algorithms. Keyword tip: 'training or tuning models from scratch' is the domain of SageMaker training jobs and SageMaker Autopilot, not Amazon Bedrock.
Amazon Bedrock is ideal for quick deployment of computer vision models, while Amazon SageMaker JumpStart specializes in deploying natural language processing models
Explanation
Incorrect. Neither Amazon Bedrock nor Amazon SageMaker JumpStart is narrowly confined to a single modality or domain. Both services are multi-modal and versatile: Amazon Bedrock supports text generation, image generation, and embedding models from diverse providers, while Amazon SageMaker JumpStart covers computer vision, NLP, tabular data, and generative AI use cases alike. Restricting either service to a single domain fundamentally misrepresents their breadth. Keyword tip: any option that constrains Amazon Bedrock or Amazon SageMaker JumpStart to a single narrow modality — such as 'only computer vision' or 'only NLP' — is an oversimplification and should be eliminated as a distractor on the MLA-C01 exam.
Amazon Bedrock is designed specifically for building and deploying custom machine learning models, while Amazon SageMaker JumpStart is tailored for deploying pre-trained large language models (LLMs) with minimal customization
Explanation
Incorrect. This option inverts the actual purpose of each service. Amazon Bedrock is not designed for building and deploying custom machine learning models from scratch; rather, it is a fully managed service focused on accessing and deploying pre-trained foundation models (FMs) from providers such as Anthropic, AI21 Labs, Cohere, and Amazon. Amazon SageMaker JumpStart, on the other hand, is not limited to LLMs — it provides a broad ML hub with pre-built solutions spanning multiple use cases, including computer vision, tabular data, and generative AI. Keyword tip: when any option attributes 'custom model building from scratch' to Amazon Bedrock rather than to SageMaker training jobs, treat it as a distractor.
Overall explanation

What this question tests

This question tests the candidate's ability to distinguish between two AWS services that both accelerate ML and generative AI adoption but serve different primary purposes. It is tied to the ML Model Development domain. 

The concepts at a glance

Amazon Bedrock focuses on providing a managed service for accessing and building applications with pre-trained foundation models from various providers, whereas Amazon SageMaker JumpStart offers a range of pre-built solutions, including models, notebooks, and algorithms for both machine learning and generative AI use cases: Amazon Bedrock is a fully managed service that provides access to high-performing foundation models from leading AI providers, including AI21 Labs, Anthropic, Cohere, Meta, Mistral AI, Stability AI, and Amazon, through a single API, making it the primary choice for building generative AI applications without managing infrastructure.

Question 50Skipped

An industrial manufacturing company, PrecisionForge Industries, is building a customer support application that provides automated responses to user queries. 

The application uses Amazon Q Business to retrieve answers to customer questions about equipment product lines.
However, the company must ensure that the API responses do not include any references to discontinued or recalled product models that are no longer supported.   

Which solution will meet this requirement? 
Correct answer
Use the blocked phrase functionality in Amazon Q Business to filter out responses containing references to discontinued product models before returning results.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon Q Business provides a built-in blocked phrase functionality as part of its guardrails and global controls. This feature is specifically designed to filter out defined terms or phrases from API responses at the application level, ensuring that references to discontinued or recalled product models are never surfaced to end users. It integrates natively with Amazon Q Business without requiring any external processing layer, minimizing operational overhead and latency. Keyword tip: When a question asks about filtering or suppressing specific output content within Amazon Q Business responses, the blocked phrase feature under Amazon Q Business global controls guardrails is the purpose-built, lowest-overhead solution.
Configure an Amazon Kendra retriever to exclude any content related to discontinued product models from the response dataset.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Kendra is an enterprise search and document retrieval service used to index and query document repositories. While it can serve as a retriever for Amazon Q Business, it is not designed to enforce phrase-level filtering within Amazon Q Business API responses. Configuring Kendra to exclude content from its index is a data-source–level operation and does not guarantee that Amazon Q Business will never surface those terms in a generated response. Keyword tip: Amazon Kendra is a distractor when the requirement is about filtering generated API responses at the output layer rather than controlling which documents are indexed for retrieval.
Set up an AWS Lambda function to process API responses from Amazon Q Business and remove any references to discontinued product models before displaying the results.
Explanation
Incorrect. Although an AWS Lambda function could technically post-process Amazon Q Business API responses and strip out unwanted references, this approach introduces unnecessary latency, additional infrastructure complexity, and higher operational overhead. Amazon Q Business already provides a native blocked phrase mechanism that handles this requirement directly at the API level, making a Lambda-based workaround redundant and suboptimal. Keyword tip: Custom Lambda post-processing is a distractor when a managed, built-in guardrail feature already exists within the target service that directly satisfies the stated filtering requirement.
Use Amazon Comprehend to analyze the text of API responses and block any responses containing references to discontinued product models.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Comprehend is a Natural Language Processing (NLP) service used for tasks such as entity recognition, sentiment analysis, and key phrase extraction. It does not have a native integration to intercept and block Amazon Q Business API responses, and implementing such a flow would require additional custom logic and infrastructure. It is not the appropriate tool for enforcing output filtering within a managed conversational AI service like Amazon Q Business. Keyword tip: Amazon Comprehend is a distractor when the task involves blocking or filtering responses from a managed conversational AI service rather than performing standalone, decoupled text analysis.
Overall explanation

What this question tests

This question tests knowledge of Amazon Q Business guardrails, specifically the blocked phrase functionality available through its global controls. It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

- Use the blocked phrase functionality in Amazon Q Business to filter out responses containing references to discontinued product models before returning results: Amazon Q Business provides a built-in blocked phrase functionality as part of its guardrails and global controls.

Question 51Skipped

A commercial real estate firm, PropAccess Realty Group, operates a network of secured facilities including data rooms, vault storage areas, and restricted tenant floors. Cameras installed at entry points upload images to an Amazon S3 bucket.

The firm has a limited number of images of authorized personnel, making it impractical to train a custom facial recognition model from scratch. The solution must minimize operational overhead while accurately identifying authorized staff members. The firm also wants to store and manage the data for audit purposes. 


Which of the following options must be combined to develop a solution that meets these requirements? (Select TWO.)

Correct selection
Set up an AWS Lambda function to trigger a face match operation when new images are uploaded to the S3 bucket. Write the results to an Amazon DynamoDB table for auditing
Explanation
Correct. By integrating Amazon Rekognition's SearchFacesByImage API with AWS Lambda, the workflow is fully automated: whenever a new image is uploaded to the Amazon S3 bucket, the Lambda function is triggered to perform a face match operation against the pre-built Rekognition face collection. Match results — including confidence scores and matched personnel identifiers — are then written to an Amazon DynamoDB table, creating a scalable, low-latency, serverless audit trail without requiring custom model training or manual intervention. Keyword tip: 'Lambda + Rekognition SearchFacesByImage + DynamoDB' is the canonical serverless pattern for automated facial identification with durable audit logging on AWS.
Set up an AWS Lambda function to trigger a face detection operation when new images are uploaded to the S3 bucket. Write the results to an Amazon DynamoDB table for auditing
Explanation
Incorrect. Face detection using the Amazon Rekognition DetectFaces API only identifies the presence, location, and attributes — such as facial landmarks, pose, or estimated emotions — of faces within an image. It does not compare or match detected faces against a stored collection of known individuals. For access control and personnel identification, face matching via the SearchFacesByImage API against a pre-indexed face collection is required, not mere detection. Keyword tip: 'Face detection' identifies that a face exists in an image, whereas 'face matching' identifies whose face it is — a critical distinction for access control scenarios.
Correct selection
Use Amazon Rekognition to create a face collection for authorized personnel
Explanation
Correct. Amazon Rekognition face collections allow you to index faces of known individuals by storing their feature vectors, which are generated from a small number of provided reference images. When a new entry-point image is submitted, Rekognition compares it against the indexed vectors in the collection using optimized machine learning algorithms, returning identity matches with confidence scores. This approach handles limited training data gracefully — only a small number of reference images per person are needed — and requires no custom model training, directly addressing the firm's constraint of having few images of authorized personnel. Keyword tip: 'Face collection' and 'IndexFaces API' in Amazon Rekognition represent the managed, pre-trained solution for face indexing and identity matching without custom model training.
Use Amazon Lookout for Vision to create custom labels for authorized personnel
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Lookout for Vision is a machine learning service purpose-built for detecting visual anomalies and defects in manufactured or processed products at scale. It is not designed for facial recognition or physical access control. This service does not support face collection, face indexing, or face matching capabilities of any kind. Keyword tip: When you see 'Lookout for Vision,' associate it with industrial or product defect detection use cases, not biometric identification or personnel verification.
Use Amazon Rekognition to create custom labels for authorized personnel
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Rekognition Custom Labels is designed for identifying domain-specific objects, scenes, logos, or visual content in images — not for facial recognition or identity verification. It requires a labeled training dataset to build a custom model and does not support creating face collections for person identification. For personnel authentication use cases, the face collection feature of Amazon Rekognition is the appropriate tool. Keyword tip: 'Custom Labels' relates to object and scene classification workflows, not face identity matching or access control.
Overall explanation

Question 52Skipped

A healthcare diagnostics provider, MedVision Analytics, relies on an ML model to forecast daily diagnostic imaging workloads, which helps manage processing infrastructure across its regional diagnostic centers.

The model runs once every evening to generate predictions for the next day. It uses workload data from the past few days as input with a maximum payload size of 3.5 MB, and the prediction process completes within 60 seconds.

What is the most suitable deployment option on Amazon SageMaker to meet these requirements? 
Correct answer
Use a serverless inference endpoint, with the MaxConcurrency parameter set to 1
Explanation

Correct. Amazon SageMaker Serverless Inference is purpose-built for workloads with idle periods between traffic spurts and intermittent or unpredictable request patterns. It automatically provisions and scales compute resources without requiring instance type selection or scaling policy management. Critically, it supports payload sizes up to 4 MB (accommodating the 3.5 MB requirement) and processing times up to 60 seconds (matching the scenario's completion window). Setting MaxConcurrency to 1 ensures the endpoint handles one request at a time, which is entirely sufficient for a single daily inference run, and the pay-per-use model eliminates costs during the remaining idle hours each day. Keyword tip: 'intermittent traffic + no infrastructure management + pay only for use + payload ≤ 4 MB + processing time ≤ 60 s' → Serverless Inference.
Use a Real-time inference endpoint, with the minimum number of copies set to 1
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Real-time inference endpoints are ideal for persistent, low-latency, or high-throughput workloads that require a continuously available REST API backed by dedicated instances. This scenario requires inference only once per day, meaning the endpoint would sit idle for approximately 23 or more hours per day while still incurring instance costs. Real-time endpoints do not natively scale to zero, making this option cost-inefficient for a once-daily forecasting job with no sustained traffic requirements. Keyword tip: 'sustained traffic, low latency, high throughput, persistent endpoint' → Real-time Inference; avoid for infrequent or bursty inference patterns.
Use an Asynchronous Inference endpoint and place the request payload in Amazon S3
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference is designed for requests with large payload sizes (up to 1 GB), long processing times (up to one hour), and near-real-time latency requirements where requests can be queued. While it does support cost savings through scale-to-zero autoscaling, none of its distinguishing characteristics — very large payloads, extended processing durations, or queue-based architecture — are relevant to this scenario. The payload (3.5 MB) and processing time (60 seconds) fall well within Serverless Inference limits, making Asynchronous Inference an unnecessarily complex and over-engineered solution for this use case. Keyword tip: 'payload up to 1 GB, processing up to 1 hour, queue-based architecture' → Asynchronous Inference, not Serverless.
Use Batch Transform for inference with Amazon SageMaker
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform is designed for offline, large-scale inference where data is available upfront and processed in bulk without requiring a persistent endpoint. While the scenario involves a once-daily inference run, the payload is a small 3.5 MB input rather than a large offline dataset stored in Amazon S3, and the use case is generating a single next-day forecast — not bulk scoring of millions of records. Batch Transform is better suited for scenarios involving large files or high-volume datasets that need periodic scoring without a live endpoint, not for single small-payload prediction requests. Keyword tip: 'large offline datasets, no persistent endpoint, bulk scoring from S3' → Batch Transform.
Overall explanation

What this question tests 

This question tests the candidate's ability to select the appropriate Amazon SageMaker inference deployment option based on traffic pattern, payload size, processing time, and cost efficiency. It is tied to the Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows domain. 

The concepts at a glance 

- Use a serverless inference endpoint, with the MaxConcurrency parameter set to 1: Amazon SageMaker Serverless Inference is purpose-built for workloads with idle periods between traffic spurts and intermittent or unpredictable request patterns.

- Use an Asynchronous Inference endpoint and place the request payload in Amazon S3: Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference is designed for requests with large payload sizes (up to 1 GB), long processing times (up to one hour), and near-real-time latency requirements where requests can be queued.

- Use a Real-time inference endpoint, with the minimum number of copies set to 1: Amazon SageMaker Real-time inference endpoints are ideal for persistent, low-latency, or high-throughput workloads that require a continuously available REST API backed by dedicated instances.

- Use Batch Transform for inference with Amazon SageMaker: Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform is designed for offline, large-scale inference where data is available upfront and processed in bulk without requiring a persistent endpoint. 

Why Use a serverless inference endpoint, with the MaxConcurrency parameter set to 1 fits this scenario Critically, it supports payload sizes up to 4 MB (accommodating the 3.5 MB requirement) and processing times up to 60 seconds (matching the scenario's completion window).

Key exam terms: SageMaker Serverless Inference, MaxConcurrency, intermittent inference, pay-per-use, payload size limit, Batch Transform, Asynchronous Inference, Real-time endpoint 

Exam tip 'intermittent traffic + no infrastructure management + pay only for use + payload ≤ 4 MB + processing time ≤ 60 s' → Serverless Inference.

Question 53Skipped

A data scientist at BuildCore Engineering, a large construction and engineering firm, needs to analyze historical project cost and procurement patterns by running ML models on extensive historical datasets spanning multiple years. 
The analysis must be conducted in an asynchronous manner to handle the large data volume efficiently.

Additionally, the firm wants to monitor the data quality of models over time to detect any degradation in input data characteristics. The firm must receive a notification if any significant change in data quality is detected.

Which of the following solutions should the data scientist implement to meet these requirements?
Use Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform to deploy the model. Set up SageMaker Data Wrangler to track data quality and configure it to send alerts for any significant changes.
Explanation
Incorrect. While Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform is the correct deployment choice for asynchronous, large-scale inference, SageMaker Data Wrangler is a data preparation and feature engineering tool designed for use during the data exploration and pre-training phase — not for monitoring production model quality post-deployment. Data Wrangler cannot continuously evaluate statistical drift in live inference data, establish a production baseline, or trigger alerting mechanisms after a model is deployed. Keyword tip: Data Wrangler = interactive data preparation and feature engineering before training; Model Monitor = continuous statistical drift detection in production.
Use Docker to containerize the ML model and deploy it on AWS Lambda. Configure AWS CloudTrail to monitor data quality and send alerts.
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS CloudTrail records API calls and user activity for governance, compliance, and operational auditing purposes — it does not monitor the statistical properties of ML model input data or detect data quality drift. Furthermore, AWS Lambda has strict execution time limits (15 minutes maximum) and memory constraints that make it fundamentally unsuitable for running ML inference on large, multi-year historical datasets. These two services combined address security auditing, not ML data quality monitoring. Keyword tip: whenever CloudTrail is paired with ML model quality monitoring in an answer option, eliminate it immediately — CloudTrail = API audit logging, not model monitoring.
Correct answer
Use Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform to deploy the model. Set up SageMaker Model Monitor to track data quality and configure it to send alerts for any significant changes.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform is the appropriate deployment option for asynchronous inference on large datasets — it provisions compute only for the duration of the job, does not require a persistent endpoint, efficiently processes high-volume historical data, and automatically associates input records with their corresponding inference outputs. SageMaker Model Monitor continuously evaluates the statistical properties of incoming production data against a pre-established baseline, detects data quality drift, and natively integrates with Amazon CloudWatch and Amazon SNS to deliver alerts when significant deviations are detected. Together, these two services satisfy both the asynchronous inference and the ongoing data quality monitoring requirements. Keyword tip: large datasets + asynchronous inference + statistical drift detection + alerting = Batch Transform + Model Monitor.
Use Amazon SageMaker Real-time inference to deploy the model. Set up SageMaker Model Monitor to track data quality and configure it to send alerts for any significant changes.
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Real-time inference is designed for interactive, low-latency, synchronous workloads that require a persistent, always-on endpoint. It is not appropriate for processing large historical datasets that span multiple years, where an asynchronous, batch-oriented approach is far more cost-effective and efficient. Although SageMaker Model Monitor is correctly identified here as the monitoring tool, the inference deployment choice is wrong given the large-volume, asynchronous requirement. Keyword tip: Real-time inference = low latency + persistent endpoint + synchronous; Batch Transform = large datasets + no persistent endpoint + asynchronous processing.
Overall explanation

What this question tests

This question tests the ability to select the correct SageMaker inference mode and the appropriate post-deployment monitoring tool for a large-scale, asynchronous MLOps scenario. It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

- Use Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform to deploy the model. Question 54Skipped

A data science team at a renewable energy company called SolarGrid Analytics is working on a machine learning project that requires ingesting large volumes of raw sensor and grid performance data for analysis and feature engineering.

The team plans to use Amazon SageMaker for the project, and they need to efficiently ingest the data into a platform where they can perform transformations and store features for future model training. 

Which of the following approaches is the most efficient for ingesting data, performing data transformations, and then storing the engineered features? 
Load the data into an Amazon RDS database, then use SageMaker Clarify to ingest and transform the data for storing in the Feature Store
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon RDS is a relational database service designed for structured transactional workloads, not as a data ingestion layer for large-scale ML feature engineering pipelines. More critically, SageMaker Clarify is a tool for bias detection, model explainability, and fairness analysis — it has no capability to perform general-purpose data transformations on raw sensor or grid data. The correct tool for transformation prior to Feature Store ingestion is SageMaker Data Wrangler. Keyword tip: When you see 'SageMaker Clarify' paired with 'data transformation,' treat it as a distractor — Clarify is strictly for bias detection and explainability, not ETL or feature engineering.
Ingest the data into AWS Glue for transformations, then store the transformed data in Amazon S3 and load it into Amazon SageMaker using the AWS SDK for Python
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Glue is a valid ETL service and can perform data transformations at scale; however, this option stores the transformed data in Amazon S3 and loads it into SageMaker via the AWS SDK for Python — it does not persist the engineered features in a dedicated feature store. The absence of SageMaker Feature Store in this workflow means there is no centralized, versioned, and reusable feature repository available for future model training runs, which fails the stated requirement. Keyword tip: When the question explicitly requires engineered features to be stored for future model training reuse, the absence of SageMaker Feature Store in an answer option is a strong disqualifying signal regardless of the ETL service used.
Use Amazon S3 to store the raw data, then directly connect SageMaker Feature Store to ingest and transform the data
Explanation
Incorrect. While Amazon S3 is a standard and appropriate landing zone for raw data, SageMaker Feature Store is not designed to perform data transformations. It is architected to store and serve pre-engineered features, not to execute complex transformation logic on raw input data. Although Feature Store supports basic feature processing at ingest time (such as count aggregates or time-window features), performing complex transformations on large-scale datasets requires a dedicated transformation tool such as SageMaker Data Wrangler before features are written to the store. Keyword tip: 'SageMaker Feature Store' is a feature storage and retrieval system, not a transformation engine — whenever a question requires complex preprocessing before storage, Feature Store alone is insufficient.
Correct answer
Ingest the data into Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler, perform transformations, and then store the transformed data in SageMaker Feature Store
Explanation
Correct. SageMaker Data Wrangler is specifically designed to streamline data ingestion and transformation through a visual, low-code interface. It supports connections to multiple data sources, provides over 300 built-in transformations, and natively integrates with SageMaker Feature Store for direct export of engineered features. Storing features in SageMaker Feature Store ensures they are versioned, catalogued, and efficiently retrievable for both online inference (low-latency retrieval) and offline model training (batch retrieval from Amazon S3). This end-to-end integration between Data Wrangler and Feature Store represents the canonical AWS pattern for feature engineering within the SageMaker ecosystem. Keyword tip: 'large-scale raw data + transformations + store for future training' → Data Wrangler → Feature Store is the MLA-C01 reference pattern for feature engineering pipelines.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the candidate's knowledge of the recommended AWS pattern for feature engineering and feature management within the SageMaker ecosystem.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Ingest the data into Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler, perform transformations, and then store the transformed data in SageMaker Feature Store: SageMaker Data Wrangler is designed to streamline data ingestion and transformation through a visual, low-code interface. SageMaker Feature Store is designed to store, manage, and serve engineered features for future training and inference workflows.

    Load the data into an Amazon RDS database, then use SageMaker Clarify to ingest and transform the data for storing in the Feature Store: Amazon RDS is a relational database service designed for structured transactional workloads, not as the preferred ingestion layer for large-scale ML feature engineering pipelines. SageMaker Clarify is used for bias detection and model explainability, not for general feature transformation or feature ingestion.

    Use Amazon S3 to store the raw data, then directly connect SageMaker Feature Store to ingest and transform the data: Amazon S3 is a standard and appropriate landing zone for raw data. However, SageMaker Feature Store is not designed to perform data transformations. It stores and serves already engineered features.

    Ingest the data into AWS Glue for transformations, then store the transformed data in Amazon S3 and load it into Amazon SageMaker using the AWS SDK for Python: AWS Glue is a valid ETL service and can perform data transformations at scale. However, this option stores the transformed data in Amazon S3 and loads it into SageMaker through the AWS SDK for Python. It does not persist the engineered features in a dedicated feature store for reuse, versioning, and consistent access.

Why Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler and SageMaker Feature Store fit this scenario.

SageMaker Data Wrangler is the right service for preparing and transforming raw data with minimal code.

It provides a visual interface for data ingestion, cleaning, transformation, and feature engineering.

After the features are engineered, SageMaker Feature Store is the right service for storing and managing those features.

Feature Store provides a centralized repository that supports feature reuse across multiple models and teams.

It also supports both online and offline access patterns.

This makes the Data Wrangler → Feature Store flow the recommended SageMaker-native pattern for feature engineering pipelines.

It separates responsibilities clearly:

    Feature transformation: Use SageMaker Data Wrangler.

    Feature storage and reuse: Use SageMaker Feature Store.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Large-scale raw data + transformations + store for future training = Data Wrangler → Feature Store.

Remember:

    Data transformation and feature engineering: Use SageMaker Data Wrangler.

    Centralized feature repository: Use SageMaker Feature Store.

    Bias detection and explainability: Use SageMaker Clarify.

    Raw data landing zone: Use Amazon S3.

    General ETL at scale: Use AWS Glue.

Official AWS references.

    Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler.

    Amazon SageMaker Feature Store.

Question 55Skipped

An industrial manufacturing company, PrecisionForge Industries, needs to fine-tune a large language model (LLM) for a technical document summarization application.

The dataset includes anonymized equipment maintenance reports and operational logs stored in Amazon S3. The company requires a low-code/no-code (LCNC) solution to simplify the fine-tuning process while ensuring scalability and fast deployment.

The solution should leverage Amazon SageMaker capabilities to minimize manual coding and operational overhead.

Which of the following Amazon SageMaker services can be used to implement the solution? (Select THREE.)
Amazon SageMaker Clarify
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Clarify is designed to help teams detect bias in datasets and model predictions and to generate feature attribution explanations using SHAP values. It does not provide any low-code/no-code capability for fine-tuning LLMs or orchestrating model training pipelines. Although Clarify is a valuable post-training and monitoring tool, it plays no role in the model development or fine-tuning workflow described in this scenario. Keyword tip: Clarify maps to bias detection and explainability — not to model building or LCNC fine-tuning workflows. Eliminate it whenever the scenario's primary requirement is model training or development automation.
Correct selection
Amazon SageMaker Canvas
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker Canvas is a no-code machine learning tool designed specifically for business analysts and non-technical users to build and deploy ML models without writing any code. It provides an intuitive point-and-click interface for data import, dataset exploration, and automated model training, connecting natively to data sources such as Amazon S3 and Amazon Redshift. Canvas's built-in AutoML capabilities handle algorithm selection, feature engineering, and model training automatically, and its recent generative AI integrations extend its applicability to LLM-based use cases. This makes Canvas an ideal LCNC solution for manufacturing teams without deep ML expertise. Keyword tip: Whenever you see 'no-code,' 'business analyst,' or 'point-and-click ML interface,' Canvas is the intended answer in MLA-C01 LCNC scenarios.
Correct selection
Amazon SageMaker Autopilot
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker Autopilot offers low-code machine learning capabilities by automating the entire process of building, training, and tuning models while maintaining full transparency into each model-building step. Users supply a dataset and define the target variable, and Autopilot automatically preprocesses the data, selects appropriate algorithms, and optimizes hyperparameters. Notably, Autopilot generates editable Jupyter notebooks, allowing practitioners to review, customize, and refine the models further if needed, striking a balance between automation and control. This positions Autopilot as a strong LCNC tool for organizations with limited ML engineering resources, such as PrecisionForge Industries. Keyword tip: When you see 'automated model building with transparency,' 'AutoML pipeline,' and 'minimal coding,' Autopilot is a strong candidate for MLA-C01 LCNC model development scenarios.
Correct selection
Amazon SageMaker JumpStart
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker JumpStart is the most directly relevant service for leveraging LLMs in a low-code/no-code approach. JumpStart provides a curated catalog of pre-trained foundation models and LLMs — including models for text summarization, text generation, and question answering — that can be fine-tuned on custom datasets with minimal effort directly from the Amazon SageMaker Studio interface. Users can upload a domain-specific dataset, such as PrecisionForge Industries' maintenance reports, and configure fine-tuning parameters through a guided UI without writing custom training code, enabling rapid iteration and deployment of task-specific LLMs. Keyword tip: Whenever the scenario involves 'LLM fine-tuning,' 'pre-trained foundation models,' or 'no-code NLP deployment on custom data,' JumpStart is the primary correct answer for MLA-C01.
Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor is a production-monitoring service that continuously tracks the quality and performance of deployed ML models by detecting data drift, model quality degradation, and bias drift in live endpoints. It integrates with Amazon CloudWatch to trigger alerts when metrics fall outside defined thresholds. Model Monitor plays no role in the fine-tuning or initial development of LLMs and provides no low-code/no-code model-building capability. Keyword tip: Model Monitor appears in monitoring and maintenance domain scenarios — eliminate it immediately when the question focuses on model development, fine-tuning, or LCNC training workflows.
Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler is a data preparation and feature engineering tool integrated into Amazon SageMaker Studio that provides a visual interface for importing, transforming, and analyzing tabular and unstructured data before model training. While it simplifies data preprocessing workflows and reduces data engineering code, it does not provide any LCNC model-building, LLM fine-tuning, or automated training capability. It addresses the data preparation stage of the ML lifecycle rather than the model development stage described in this scenario. Keyword tip: Data Wrangler belongs to the data preparation phase of the ML pipeline — eliminate it when the scenario's core requirement is model fine-tuning, training automation, or LCNC model development rather than data transformation.
Overall explanation

What this question tests 

This question tests knowledge of Amazon SageMaker's low-code/no-code (LCNC) ecosystem and which services are appropriate for fine-tuning LLMs and building ML models with minimal manual coding in an industrial manufacturing context. It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance 

- Amazon SageMaker Autopilot: Amazon SageMaker Autopilot offers low-code machine learning capabilities by automating the entire process of building, training, and tuning models while maintaining full transparency into each model-building step.

- Amazon SageMaker Canvas: Amazon SageMaker Canvas is a no-code machine learning tool designed specifically for business analysts and non-technical users to build and deploy ML models without writing any code. - Amazon SageMaker JumpStart: Amazon SageMaker JumpStart is the most directly relevant service for leveraging LLMs in a low-code/no-code approach.

- Amazon SageMaker Clarify: Amazon SageMaker Clarify is designed to help teams detect bias in datasets and model predictions and to generate feature attribution explanations using SHAP values. - Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor: Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor is a production-monitoring service that continuously tracks the quality and performance of deployed ML models by detecting data drift, model quality degradation, and bias drift in live endpoints.

- Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler: Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler is a data preparation and feature engineering tool integrated into Amazon SageMaker Studio that provides a visual interface for importing, transforming, and analyzing tabular and unstructured data before model training. Question 56Skipped

A data scientist at Meridian Community Bank has developed a binary classification model for credit risk assessment using an external framework and stored the model artifacts in an Amazon S3 bucket. 

The scientist now wants to make the model available to a colleague using Amazon SageMaker Canvas for further tuning and analysis. 

Both the data scientist and the colleague are part of the same SageMaker domain.

What requirements must be fulfilled to ensure the model can be shared with the SageMaker Canvas user?   (Select TWO.)
Correct selection
The Canvas user must have permissions to access the S3 bucket where the model artifacts are stored.
Explanation
Correct. The SageMaker Canvas user with whom you want to share the model must have the necessary IAM permissions to access the Amazon S3 bucket containing the datasets and model artifacts. Without adequate S3 access, the Canvas user cannot retrieve or utilize the externally developed model artifacts, regardless of whether the model is registered in the Model Registry. Keyword tip: 'Canvas BYOM + S3 permissions' — S3 bucket access for the Canvas user is a mandatory, independently stated prerequisite when bringing your own model into Canvas; never assume it is implied by domain membership alone.
The Canvas user must be configured in the SageMaker domain. The Canvas user must have permissions to access the S3 bucket where the model artifacts are stored.
Explanation
Incorrect. This option acts as a distractor by bundling a redundant condition with a correct one. The scenario explicitly states that both users are already part of the same SageMaker domain, so asserting that the Canvas user must be configured in the domain adds no new requirement. Selecting this composite option instead of option 4 alone misrepresents the actual prerequisites and obscures the true two requirements. Keyword tip: Watch for options that combine an already-satisfied scenario condition with a genuinely correct requirement — this bundling technique is designed to mislead candidates into selecting an option that partially duplicates the scenario's stated facts.
The Canvas user must be configured to the SageMaker domain. Autopilot should be used to share the ML model with SageMaker Canvas.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Autopilot automates the process of exploring data, selecting algorithms, and training and tuning models for AutoML workflows. It cannot be used to import externally built models into SageMaker Canvas, and there is no mechanism by which Autopilot facilitates sharing of external model artifacts. Using Autopilot to share an externally developed credit risk model into Canvas is not a valid requirement or approach. Keyword tip: When 'Autopilot' is paired with importing external models into Canvas, treat it as a distractor — Autopilot is for automated model creation, not for bridging external artifacts into Canvas.
Correct selection
The model must be registered in the SageMaker Model Registry.
Explanation
Correct. For any model built outside of SageMaker AI using an external framework, the model must be registered in the SageMaker Model Registry before it can be imported into SageMaker Canvas via the Bring Your Own Model (BYOM) feature. The Model Registry serves as the required integration gateway for externally developed models entering the Canvas environment. Keyword tip: 'External framework + Canvas import' always requires Model Registry registration as a prerequisite — this is the key signal on the MLA-C01 exam for BYOM workflows involving Canvas.
The latest version of Amazon SageMaker Studio is needed; SageMaker Studio Classic does not support sharing models to Canvas.
Explanation
Incorrect. This option reverses the actual requirement. Sharing models to Canvas — or viewing models shared from Canvas — is currently supported only in SageMaker Studio Classic, not in the latest version of SageMaker Studio independently. If you are using the latest SageMaker Studio, you must launch Studio Classic from within it to perform model sharing with Canvas. Keyword tip: 'Studio Classic required for Canvas model sharing' — when a question contrasts the latest SageMaker Studio with Studio Classic in a Canvas collaboration context, Studio Classic is the supported interface, making any claim that the latest Studio alone suffices incorrect.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of the prerequisites for the SageMaker Canvas Bring Your Own Model (BYOM) feature in an external-framework scenario.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

    The Canvas user must have permissions to access the S3 bucket where the model artifacts are stored: The SageMaker Canvas user with whom the model is shared must have the necessary IAM permissions to access the Amazon S3 bucket that contains the datasets and model artifacts.

Autopilot should be used to share the ML model with SageMaker Canvas: Amazon SageMaker Autopilot automates data exploration, algorithm selection, model training, and tuning for AutoML workflows.     The latest version of Amazon SageMaker Studio is needed; SageMaker Studio Classic does not support sharing models to Canvas: This option reverses the actual requirement. The key prerequisite for the external-framework model itself is still Model Registry registration.

The Canvas user with whom the model is shared must also have the necessary IAM permissions to access the Amazon S3 bucket that contains the datasets and model artifacts.

Question 57Skipped

You are a machine learning engineer at AxleDrive Motors, an automotive manufacturer working on a deep learning model to analyze sensor sequence data derived from vehicle telematics and component diagnostics.

The model requires significant computational resources for training due to the complexity and size of the dataset, which consists of billions of telemetry records collected from the vehicle fleet.

Additionally, once deployed, the model must provide real-time inferences for onboard quality-control and predictive-maintenance applications, requiring low-latency predictions.

Your task is to choose the appropriate compute environment for both training and inference. 

Which of the following compute environment configurations is the MOST SUITABLE for meeting the training and inference requirements of this use case?   
Use Amazon EC2 with g4dn instances (NVIDIA T4 GPUs) for both training and inference, optimizing for cost-efficiency while maintaining moderate GPU performance.
Explanation
Incorrect. g4dn instances with NVIDIA T4 GPUs are cost-efficient and adequate for moderate inference and smaller training workloads, but the T4 GPU is architecturally positioned for inference optimization rather than large-scale distributed training. For a dataset of billions of records requiring high-throughput training, g4dn instances are significantly underpowered compared to p4d. Furthermore, running both training and inference on Amazon EC2 directly forgoes the purpose-built inference optimization that ml.inf1 delivers, and it sacrifices SageMaker's managed training infrastructure advantages. Keyword tip: 'g4dn for cost-efficiency' is the canonical distractor when a scenario explicitly demands high-performance large-scale training combined with low-latency production inference.
Correct answer
Use Amazon SageMaker with p4d instances (NVIDIA A100 GPUs) for training to handle large-scale deep learning workloads, and deploy the model using ml.inf1 instances (AWS Inferentia chips) for low-latency inference.
Explanation
Correct. p4d instances equipped with NVIDIA A100 GPUs represent the highest-performance SageMaker training instance tier, specifically engineered for large-scale deep learning workloads requiring extreme parallelism, NVLink interconnects, and high memory bandwidth — ideal for training on billions of telemetry records. For inference, ml.inf1 instances powered by AWS Inferentia chips are purpose-built for high-throughput, low-latency deep learning inference at a reduced cost-per-inference compared to GPU-based inference instances. This combination directly satisfies both the compute-intensive training requirement and the real-time low-latency inference requirement for AxleDrive Motors' predictive-maintenance application. Keyword tip: 'p4d + ml.inf1' is the canonical MLA-C01 answer pattern when a scenario combines large-scale deep learning training with production low-latency inference requirements on Amazon SageMaker.
Use Amazon SageMaker with ml.c5.large instances (Intel Cascade Lake CPUs) for training and inference, relying on the CPU's high memory bandwidth for both tasks.
Explanation
Incorrect. ml.c5.large instances with Intel Cascade Lake CPUs are compute-optimized general-purpose instances with no GPU acceleration whatsoever. They are fundamentally unsuitable for training large-scale deep learning models on billions of telemetry records, and they lack the specialized hardware required for low-latency inference on complex neural networks. Applying the same CPU instance family to both training and inference compounds the performance deficiency for this workload profile. Keyword tip: 'ml.c5' appearing in a deep learning training context is a strong distractor signal on MLA-C01 — CPU-only instances cannot satisfy the computational demands of large-scale deep learning workloads.
Use Amazon SageMaker with p3 instances (NVIDIA V100 GPUs) for training, and deploy the model using ml.m5.large instances (Intel Skylake CPUs) for real-time inference to balance cost and performance.
Explanation
Incorrect. While p3 instances with NVIDIA V100 GPUs provide solid deep learning training performance, ml.m5.large instances with Intel Skylake CPUs are general-purpose compute instances not optimized for deep learning inference. CPU-based endpoints introduce higher latency and lower throughput when serving large neural networks, making them unsuitable for real-time, low-latency predictive-maintenance applications at fleet scale. The training compute is reasonable but the inference tier is the critical flaw in this pairing. Keyword tip: When you see 'ml.m5' paired with a deep learning inference requirement in a low-latency scenario, treat it as a distractor — general-purpose CPU instances create a throughput bottleneck for large neural network serving.
Overall explanation

What this question tests

This question tests the ability to select appropriate Amazon SageMaker instance types that match the distinct computational profiles required for training versus inference in a large-scale deep learning scenario. It is tied to the Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows domain.

- Use Amazon SageMaker with p4d instances (NVIDIA A100 GPUs) for training to handle large-scale deep learning workloads, and deploy the model using ml.inf1 instances (AWS Inferentia chips) for low-latency inference: p4d instances equipped with NVIDIA A100 GPUs represent the highest-performance SageMaker training instance tier, specifically engineered for large-scale deep learning workloads requiring extreme parallelism, NVLink interconnects, and high memory bandwidth, such as large-scale distributed training.

- Use Amazon SageMaker with p3 instances (NVIDIA V100 GPUs) for training, and deploy the model using ml.m5.large instances (Intel Skylake CPUs) for real-time inference to balance cost and performance: While p3 instances with NVIDIA V100 GPUs provide solid deep learning training performance, ml.m5.large instances with Intel Skylake CPUs are general-purpose compute instances not optimized for deep learning inference. - Use Amazon SageMaker with ml.c5.large instances (Intel Cascade Lake CPUs) for training and inference, relying on the CPU's high memory bandwidth for both tasks: ml.c5.large instances with Intel Cascade Lake CPUs are compute-optimized general-purpose instances with no GPU acceleration whatsoever. -

Why Use Amazon SageMaker with p4d instances (NVIDIA A100 GPUs) for training to handle large-scale deep learning workloads, and deploy the model using ml.inf1 instances (AWS Inferentia chips) for low-latency inference fits this scenario This combination directly satisfies both the compute-intensive training requirement and the real-time low-latency inference requirement for AxleDrive Motors' predictive-maintenance application. 

Exam tip 'p4d + ml.inf1' is the canonical MLA-C01 answer pattern when a scenario combines large-scale deep learning training with production low-latency inference requirements on Amazon SageMaker.

Question 58Skipped

A data scientist at StructaCore Engineering, a large construction and engineering firm, has deployed a sentiment analysis machine learning model to an Amazon SageMaker endpoint.

Given the involvement of multiple stakeholders — including project managers, compliance officers, and executive leadership — the data scientist needs to explain how the model generates its predictions and clarify the factors influencing the model's decisions. 

Which solution will provide an explanation for the model's predictions?
Correct answer
Configure a SageMaker Clarify processing job to analyze the model for bias and explainability.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker Clarify is the purpose-built AWS service for model fairness and explainability. By configuring a SageMaker Clarify processing job, you can compute bias metrics, generate feature attribution reports using SHAP (SHapley Additive exPlanations) values, and produce human-readable explainability reports that identify which input features most influenced a model's predictions — both during training and at inference time. This directly satisfies the requirement to explain how StructaCore Engineering's model generates its predictions and to provide transparency to diverse stakeholders such as compliance officers and project managers. Keyword tip: SageMaker Clarify + SHAP values + feature attribution + stakeholder transparency = model explainability use case on the MLA-C01 exam.
Use Amazon Textract to automatically extract text and data from the ML model and provide insights on its predictions.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Textract is a fully managed document analysis service that extracts text, handwriting, key-value pairs, tables, and selection elements from scanned documents and images. It has no capability to inspect, interpret, or explain the internal workings of a deployed machine learning model. It is entirely unrelated to model explainability, feature attribution, or bias analysis in the context of a SageMaker endpoint. Keyword tip: Textract = OCR and structured document extraction; any option invoking Textract for ML model introspection is a distractor on the MLA-C01 exam.
Use Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor, which offers tools to provide explanations on model quality.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor is designed to continuously monitor the quality of deployed machine learning models in real time, enabling automated alerts when deviations such as data drift, model drift, or anomalies are detected. However, it does not compute feature attribution scores or explain why the model made a specific individual prediction — those capabilities belong to SageMaker Clarify. Configuring Model Monitor alone would not satisfy the requirement to clarify prediction-influencing factors for multiple stakeholders. Keyword tip: When a scenario mentions monitoring for drift or quality degradation over time — rather than explaining individual predictions — SageMaker Model Monitor is the signal, not SageMaker Clarify.
Leverage CloudWatch Insights derived from SageMaker endpoint invocation metrics to analyze the model for bias and explainability.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon CloudWatch can collect operational invocation metrics from SageMaker endpoints — such as latency, error rates, and invocation counts — which are useful for infrastructure performance monitoring and setting alarms. However, these metrics reflect aggregate usage patterns at the infrastructure level and provide no insight into individual prediction logic, feature importance, or algorithmic bias. They cannot substitute for the SHAP-based feature attribution reports that SageMaker Clarify produces. Keyword tip: CloudWatch invocation metrics = operational and performance observability for SageMaker endpoints, NOT a mechanism for explainability or bias analysis.
Overall explanation

Question 59Skipped

A data science team at a consumer electronics manufacturer, NovaTech Devices, is evaluating foundational concepts in generative AI to guide their model selection strategy for upcoming product features.

What is the primary distinction between discriminative models and generative models in the context of generative AI?
Discriminative models are used to generate new data, while generative models are used only for classification
Explanation
Incorrect. This option reverses the correct roles of the two model types: discriminative models are designed for classification by learning decision boundaries between classes, not for generating new data samples. Generative models are designed to model the underlying data distribution and produce novel samples resembling training data, and while some generative classifiers exist, their primary purpose is generation rather than classification. Reversing well-defined model roles is a recurring distractor pattern on the MLA-C01 exam. Keyword tip: reversed-role distractors are common on the MLA-C01 exam; always verify that the described capability — generation vs. classification — is correctly matched to the named model type before selecting.
Generative models are trained on labeled data, while discriminative models can be trained on both labeled and unlabeled data
Explanation
Incorrect. The distinction between labeled and unlabeled training data pertains to supervised, semi-supervised, and unsupervised learning paradigms — not to the generative vs. discriminative distinction. Both generative and discriminative models can be trained using labeled data; moreover, certain generative models such as variational autoencoders (VAEs) can leverage unlabeled data to learn latent representations. Conflating training data supervision with the generative/discriminative split is a common conceptual error. Keyword tip: questions referencing 'labeled vs. unlabeled data' typically target supervised vs. unsupervised learning concepts, not the generative/discriminative divide.
Correct answer
Generative models focus on generating new data from learned patterns, whereas discriminative models classify data by distinguishing between different classes
Explanation
Correct. Generative models learn the underlying joint probability distribution of features and labels — or the data distribution P(x) directly — enabling them to synthesize new data samples that resemble the training distribution. This is exemplified by models such as GPT-3 for text generation, GANs for image synthesis, and diffusion models for multimodal content creation. Discriminative models, by contrast, learn decision boundaries to map inputs to output class labels by modeling the conditional probability P(y|x), making them well-suited for classification and regression tasks. Keyword tip: when a question asks about a model's ability to 'create new content' or 'synthesize samples' versus 'classify inputs' or 'predict labels,' the generative vs. discriminative boundary is the core concept being tested.
Discriminative models are only used for text classification, while generative models are only used for image classification
Explanation
Incorrect. Discriminative models are broadly applicable across multiple data modalities, including text classification (e.g., sentiment analysis with BERT fine-tuning) and image classification (e.g., CNNs for object recognition in product quality control), and are not limited to text tasks. Likewise, generative models are capable of producing data across diverse modalities — text, images, audio, video, and synthetic tabular data — and are not restricted to image generation. Artificially constraining model types to a single modality misrepresents the actual scope of both paradigms. Keyword tip: any answer option that restricts a model type to exactly one data modality should be flagged immediately as a distractor on the MLA-C01 exam.
Overall explanation

What this question tests

This question tests foundational ML model theory within the MLA-C01 'ML Model Development' domain, specifically the ability to differentiate between generative and discriminative modeling paradigms.

The concepts at a glance

- Generative models focus on generating new data from learned patterns, whereas discriminative models classify data by distinguishing between different classes:
Generative models learn the underlying joint probability distribution of features and labels , or the data distribution P(x) directly , enabling them to synthesize new data samples that resemble the training distribution.

- Generative models are trained on labeled data, while discriminative models can be trained on both labeled and unlabeled data: The distinction between labeled and unlabeled training data pertains to supervised, semi-supervised, and unsupervised learning paradigms , not to the generative vs. discriminative distinction. - Discriminative models are only used for text classification, while generative models are only used for image classification: Discriminative models are broadly applicable across multiple data modalities, including text classification, such as sentiment analysis using BERT fine-tuning, and image classification, such as CNN-based object recognition for product quality control. They are not limited to a single type of input data; instead, they learn decision boundaries that separate classes based on labeled examples.


- Discriminative models are used to generate new data, while generative models are used only for classification: This option reverses the correct roles of the two model types: discriminative models are designed for classification by learning decision boundaries between classes, not for generating new data samples.  Why Generative models focus on generating new data from learned patterns, whereas discriminative models classify data by distinguishing between different classes fits this scenario Generative models learn the underlying joint probability distribution of features and labels , or the data distribution P(x) directly , enabling them to synthesize new data samples that resemble the training distribution. 

Key Terms: model, discriminative model, joint probability distribution, conditional probability P(y|x), data synthesis, GANs, VAEs, diffusion models
