Question 60Skipped

A maritime shipping company uses Amazon SageMaker Studio to develop its ML models for route optimization and cargo demand forecasting. 

A team of ten developers is working on a proof-of-concept model, with all users linked to a single SageMaker Studio domain. The company needs an automated alert system that notifies them when SageMaker compute costs exceed a specific threshold. Which of the following options would meet these requirements? 

Add tags to the user profile in the SageMaker domain. Configure AWS Cost Explorer to send an alert when the threshold is breached.
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Cost Explorer provides rich visualization and analysis of AWS costs and usage trends over time, but it does not support configuring automated alerts that fire when a cost threshold is breached. For proactive, event-driven threshold-based alerting, AWS Budgets is the correct service, while Cost Explorer serves as a complementary analytical tool for post-hoc cost investigation. Keyword tip: Cost Explorer = analyze and visualize historical and forecasted costs; AWS Budgets = define thresholds and send proactive automated alerts when those thresholds are exceeded.
Add resource tagging to the IAM profile of each user. Configure AWS Trusted Advisor to track the resource costs and generate recommendations.
Explanation
Incorrect. IAM profiles do not support resource tagging for SageMaker cost tracking in this manner, as cost allocation tags must be applied at the SageMaker domain or user profile level to propagate to compute resources. Additionally, AWS Trusted Advisor provides environment inspection and best-practice recommendations across performance, security, and cost optimization categories, but it does not support configuring automated budget threshold alerts for ML compute costs. Keyword tip: AWS Trusted Advisor is designed for best-practice checks and recommendations — not for proactive cost threshold alerting; pair automated cost alerting requirements with AWS Budgets.
Add custom tags to resources by editing the SageMaker user profile in the SageMaker domain. Modify the custom tag propagation settings at the user profile level to suit each user's requirements. Configure AWS Budgets to send an alert when the threshold is breached.
Explanation
Incorrect. While adding tags to the SageMaker user profile and using AWS Budgets for alerting is the correct general approach, custom tag propagation settings can only be configured at the domain level — not at the individual user profile level. Attempting to modify tag propagation per user profile is not a supported feature in Amazon SageMaker Studio, making this option technically invalid despite its otherwise plausible structure. Keyword tip: When a scenario mentions 'user-profile-level tag propagation customization,' recognize it as an unsupported configuration that functions as a distractor — tag propagation settings belong at the SageMaker domain level.
Correct answer
Add tags to the user profile in the SageMaker domain. Configure AWS Budgets to send an alert when the threshold is breached.
Explanation
Correct. In Amazon SageMaker Studio, a user profile represents a single user within a domain and is the primary entity for sharing, reporting, and user-oriented features. When tags are added to a user profile, all resources created by that user profile automatically inherit both a domain ARN tag and a user profile ARN tag, enabling granular cost allocation per developer. AWS Budgets is the appropriate service for creating custom cost and usage budgets with automated threshold alerts, and it can also trigger Amazon SNS notifications or invoke AWS Lambda functions for automated remediation responses. Keyword tip: 'Automated cost alert upon threshold breach' in a SageMaker Studio scenario = AWS Budgets combined with SageMaker user profile tags for per-user cost allocation.
Overall explanation

Question 61Skipped

You are a DevOps engineer at VelocityDrive Motors, an automotive manufacturer that is building a scalable microservices-based application to support its connected vehicle platform.

The application is composed of several containerized services, each responsible for different functions such as driver authentication, telemetry data processing, and in-vehicle recommendation systems.

VelocityDrive Motors wants to standardize and automate the deployment and management of its infrastructure using Infrastructure as Code (IaC).

You need to choose between AWS CloudFormation and AWS Cloud Development Kit (CDK) for defining the infrastructure. Additionally, you must decide on the appropriate AWS container service to manage and deploy these microservices efficiently.

Given the requirements, which combination of IaC option and container service is MOST SUITABLE for this scenario, and why? 
Use AWS CloudFormation with YAML templates for infrastructure automation and deploy the containerized microservices using Amazon Lightsail Containers to simplify management and reduce costs.
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS CloudFormation with YAML templates is a valid IaC approach but lacks the programmatic expressiveness of AWS CDK for managing complex, dynamic infrastructure with reusable constructs, loops, and conditionals. More critically, Amazon Lightsail Containers is a simplified, low-cost container service purpose-built for small-scale or straightforward workloads, and it does not provide the orchestration capabilities, auto-scaling, or enterprise-grade features required for a complex connected vehicle microservices platform operating at scale. Keyword tip: Amazon Lightsail Containers is consistently a distractor whenever the scenario explicitly involves scalability, advanced orchestration, or enterprise-level microservices requirements.
Use AWS CloudFormation to define and deploy the infrastructure as code, and Amazon ECR (Elastic Container Registry) with Fargate for running the containerized microservices without needing to manage the underlying servers.
Explanation
Incorrect. While AWS CloudFormation is a valid IaC tool for defining infrastructure using JSON or YAML templates, Amazon ECR is a managed container image registry service — it stores and distributes container images but does not execute or orchestrate containers. Pairing ECR with Fargate as if ECR were a container runtime is technically inaccurate; the correct pairing for serverless container execution would be Amazon ECS with the Fargate launch type. This option also does not address the need for advanced Kubernetes-based orchestration required by a complex, multi-service connected vehicle platform. Keyword tip: When a distractor pairs a registry service (ECR) with a compute runtime (Fargate) as though ECR provides container execution, the option is incorrect — ECR is always a storage and distribution layer, never an orchestration layer.
Correct answer
Use AWS CDK for infrastructure as code, allowing you to define the infrastructure in a high-level programming language, and deploy the containerized microservices using Amazon EKS (Elastic Kubernetes Service) for advanced orchestration and scalability.
Explanation
Correct. AWS CDK enables engineers to define cloud infrastructure using familiar high-level programming languages such as Python, TypeScript, and Java, making it significantly easier to manage complex, dynamic infrastructure programmatically compared to static JSON or YAML templates. Amazon EKS provides Kubernetes-based container orchestration with enterprise-grade features including horizontal pod autoscaling, self-healing, rolling deployments, namespace isolation, and native CI/CD pipeline integration — all of which are essential for a complex, scalable microservices architecture such as VelocityDrive Motors' connected vehicle platform. This combination delivers both IaC expressiveness and the advanced orchestration capabilities the scenario demands. Keyword tip: CDK + EKS is the optimal combination when the scenario emphasizes high-level programming language flexibility and advanced Kubernetes-native orchestration for complex, scalable microservices.
Use AWS CDK with Amazon ECS on EC2 instances to combine the flexibility of programming languages with direct control over the underlying server infrastructure for the microservices.
Explanation
Incorrect. While AWS CDK combined with Amazon ECS on EC2 instances does provide programming language flexibility and container orchestration, managing EC2 instances introduces unnecessary operational overhead — including OS patching, capacity planning, and manual scaling configuration — compared to fully managed alternatives such as Fargate or EKS. For a scalable microservices-based application where the primary goals are automation and reduced management burden, this option adds infrastructure complexity that directly contradicts the stated requirements of VelocityDrive Motors. Keyword tip: When a scenario prioritizes scalability and reduced infrastructure management overhead, ECS on EC2 is a distractor compared to serverless (Fargate) or Kubernetes-managed (EKS) alternatives.
Overall explanation

Question 62Skipped

You are a data scientist working on a regression model to predict athlete performance valuations across a large professional sports league for ScoutMetrics Analytics, a sports analytics company managing player contract and asset portfolios.

The dataset contains many features, including player position, years of experience, physical metrics, performance statistics, and available endorsement opportunities. After initial testing, you notice that some features have very high variance, leading to overfitting.
To address this, you are considering applying regularization to your model. You need to choose between L1 (Lasso) and L2 (Ridge) regularization. 

Given the goal of reducing overfitting while also simplifying the model by eliminating less important features, which regularization method should you choose and why?
L2 regularization, because it eliminates less important features by setting their coefficients to zero, simplifying the model
Explanation
Incorrect. This option incorrectly attributes the ability to shrink coefficients to exactly zero to L2 regularization. In reality, L2 regularization (Ridge) penalizes the sum of squared coefficients, which reduces all coefficients proportionally but never forces any of them to exactly zero. Feature elimination through zero coefficients is a defining characteristic of L1 (Lasso), not L2 (Ridge). Keyword tip: When you see 'eliminates features by setting coefficients to zero,' that signal always points to L1 (Lasso), never to L2 (Ridge), regardless of the domain or dataset described.
Correct answer
L1 regularization, because it can shrink some feature coefficients to zero, effectively performing feature selection
Explanation
Correct. L1 regularization (Lasso) applies a penalty proportional to the absolute value of the coefficients. This mathematical property allows it to shrink some coefficients to exactly zero, effectively removing those features from the model and performing implicit feature selection. This is especially valuable in a high-dimensional sports analytics setting where only a subset of performance metrics is expected to be truly predictive of player valuation. In Amazon SageMaker's Linear Learner algorithm, the regularization_type hyperparameter can be set to 'L1' to enable this behavior natively during training. Keyword tip: 'Shrink coefficients to zero' and 'feature selection' are the canonical identifiers for L1 (Lasso) regularization; when both simplifying the model and reducing overfitting are stated requirements, L1 is the correct choice.
L1 regularization, because it penalizes large coefficients more heavily, making the model less sensitive to high-variance features
Explanation
Incorrect. While L1 regularization does impose a penalty on large coefficients, this option misidentifies the primary benefit as reducing sensitivity to high-variance features. The defining and most practically significant advantage of L1 regularization in this scenario is its ability to perform automatic feature selection by driving certain coefficients to exactly zero — not merely reducing them. Furthermore, penalizing large coefficients more heavily via squared penalties is more characteristic of L2 (Ridge); L1's key differentiator is the sparsity it induces in the solution. Keyword tip: If an option describes L1 only in terms of coefficient magnitude reduction without mentioning sparsity or zero coefficients, it is likely a distractor; sparsity is L1's canonical identifier on the exam.
L2 regularization, because it evenly reduces all feature coefficients, leading to a more stable model
Explanation
Incorrect. L2 regularization (Ridge) does reduce all feature coefficients by penalizing the sum of squared weights, which can stabilize the model and is particularly beneficial when features are highly correlated. However, it cannot force any coefficient to exactly zero, so it does not eliminate features from the model. When the explicit requirement is to simplify the model by removing irrelevant features — as stated in this scenario — L2 regularization is insufficient to meet that goal. Keyword tip: 'Stable model with correlated features but no feature elimination' is the signature use case for L2 (Ridge); encountering this phrase in an option should immediately disqualify it when feature selection is an explicit requirement.
Overall explanation

What this question tests

This question tests the understanding of L1 versus L2 regularization and their distinct effects on model coefficients , a core concept in ML model development. It is tied to the ML Model Development domain. 

The concepts at a glance 

  - L1 regularization, because it can shrink some feature coefficients to zero, effectively performing feature selection: L1 regularization (Lasso) applies a penalty proportional to the absolute value of the coefficients.

- L2 regularization, because it eliminates less important features by setting their coefficients to zero, simplifying the model: This option incorrectly attributes the ability to shrink coefficients to exactly zero to L2 regularization.

- L1 regularization, because it penalizes large coefficients more heavily, making the model less sensitive to high-variance features: While L1 regularization does impose a penalty on large coefficients, this option misidentifies the primary benefit as reducing sensitivity to high-variance features.

- L2 regularization, because it evenly reduces all feature coefficients, leading to a more stable model: L2 regularization (Ridge) does reduce all feature coefficients by penalizing the sum of squared weights, which can stabilize the model and is particularly beneficial when features are highly correlated. 
Why L1 regularization, because it can shrink some feature coefficients to zero, effectively performing feature selection fits this scenario L1 regularization (Lasso) applies a penalty proportional to the absolute value of the coefficients. 

Key exam terms: L1 regularization, L2 regularization, Lasso, Ridge, feature selection, sparse coefficients, overfitting, Amazon SageMaker Linear Learner 

Exam tip 'Shrink coefficients to zero' and 'feature selection' are the canonical identifiers for L1 (Lasso) regularization; when both simplifying the model and reducing overfitting are stated requirements, L1 is the correct choice.

Question 63Skipped

A telecommunications provider, NexWave Communications, is using Amazon SageMaker to train a machine learning model on AWS infrastructure to predict network congestion patterns across its regional infrastructure. The training dataset comprises millions of files, each several megabytes in size, stored in an Amazon S3 bucket.

What immediate change can be made to quickly enhance training performance with minimal effort?
Set up an Amazon Elastic File System (Amazon EFS) and link it to the existing S3 bucket. Update the training job configuration to read data directly from the file system.
Explanation
Incorrect. While Amazon EFS can be used as a data source for SageMaker training jobs and supports shared access across multiple instances, it does not provide the same level of throughput and IOPS as Amazon FSx for Lustre. EFS is a general-purpose, scalable file system suited for workloads that do not require extreme I/O performance, such as shared configuration files or lightweight datasets. For high-throughput ML training scenarios involving millions of files — as in NexWave Communications' network congestion model — FSx for Lustre significantly outperforms EFS due to its parallel I/O architecture. Keyword tip: When a question distinguishes between EFS and FSx for Lustre in a large-scale SageMaker training context, FSx for Lustre is the correct choice whenever maximum throughput and IOPS are the primary requirements.
Enable S3 Transfer Acceleration for the existing Amazon S3 bucket. Update the training job configuration to enhance training performance by leveraging the accelerated data transfer.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon S3 Transfer Acceleration is designed to speed up content transfers between external clients and Amazon S3 over the public internet by routing traffic through AWS CloudFront edge locations. This feature is beneficial for geographically dispersed users uploading or downloading large objects from outside the AWS network. Since SageMaker training jobs run entirely within AWS infrastructure and communicate with S3 over AWS's internal high-bandwidth network, S3 Transfer Acceleration provides no meaningful performance benefit and is irrelevant for this use case. Keyword tip: S3 Transfer Acceleration is only advantageous for internet-based, cross-region transfers from remote endpoints — it is never applicable to intra-AWS data access patterns such as SageMaker training job data ingestion.
Set up an Amazon S3 access point for the existing Amazon S3 bucket. Update the training job configuration to read data directly via the access point.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon S3 access points are named network endpoints attached to S3 buckets that simplify access management by providing per-application or per-team access policies at scale. They are a governance and access-control mechanism, not a performance optimization tool, and using one does not improve data ingestion throughput or reduce I/O latency for SageMaker training jobs in any way. Introducing an S3 access point adds a configuration layer without addressing the underlying bottleneck of reading millions of files during training. Keyword tip: S3 access points are associated with multi-tenant bucket policy management and data governance — not training I/O performance. Treat this option as a distractor whenever a question's stated goal is improving ML training throughput.
Correct answer
Set up an Amazon FSx for Lustre file system and link it to the existing S3 bucket. Update the training job configuration to read data directly from the file system.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon FSx for Lustre is a fully managed, high-performance parallel file system optimized for compute-intensive workloads such as machine learning training, HPC, and large-scale data processing. It integrates natively with Amazon S3, allowing the file system to be linked to an existing S3 bucket so that data is lazily loaded on first access and can be exported back seamlessly — eliminating the need to copy data to local storage before training begins. This architecture provides sub-millisecond latencies and hundreds of gigabytes per second of aggregate throughput, making it ideal for training jobs that must ingest millions of files rapidly. Keyword tip: When a SageMaker scenario mentions 'millions of files,' 'high-throughput training,' and the need to link directly to an existing S3 bucket with minimal configuration effort, Amazon FSx for Lustre is the correct answer.
Overall explanation

What this question tests

This question tests knowledge of high-performance data ingestion strategies for Amazon SageMaker training jobs when working with large-scale datasets stored in Amazon S3.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance

Set up an Amazon FSx for Lustre file system and link it to the existing S3 bucket. Update the training job configuration to read data directly from the file system

Amazon FSx for Lustre is a fully managed, high-performance parallel file system optimized for compute-intensive workloads such as machine learning training, high performance computing (HPC), and large-scale data processing.

It can be linked to an existing Amazon S3 bucket through a data repository association, allowing files in S3 to be accessed through a high-throughput file system interface.

This is the best choice when a SageMaker training job must read a very large dataset, especially when the dataset contains many files and the training workload is limited by S3 data access throughput.

Set up an Amazon Elastic File System (Amazon EFS) and link it to the existing S3 bucket. Update the training job configuration to read data directly from the file system

Amazon EFS can be used as a shared file system for workloads that need elastic, managed NFS storage.

However, Amazon EFS does not provide the same level of throughput, IOPS, and parallel file access performance as Amazon FSx for Lustre for large-scale ML training workloads.

It is useful for general shared file storage, but it is not the optimal answer when the scenario emphasizes high-throughput training data ingestion from Amazon S3.

Set up an Amazon S3 access point for the existing Amazon S3 bucket. Update the training job configuration to read data directly through the access point

Amazon S3 access points are named network endpoints attached to S3 buckets that simplify access management by providing per-application or per-team access policies at scale.

They help with access control and policy management, but they do not convert S3 into a high-performance parallel file system.

This means S3 access points do not solve the core performance problem of ingesting millions of files rapidly during SageMaker training.

Enable S3 Transfer Acceleration for the existing Amazon S3 bucket. Update the training job configuration to enhance training performance by using accelerated data transfer

Amazon S3 Transfer Acceleration is designed to speed up content transfers between external clients and Amazon S3 over the public internet by routing traffic through AWS edge locations.

It is not designed to improve data access from SageMaker training jobs running inside AWS to S3 buckets in the same Region.

For intra-AWS ML training workloads, FSx for Lustre is the better fit when the bottleneck is high-throughput file access.

Why “Set up an Amazon FSx for Lustre file system and link it to the existing S3 bucket” fits this scenario

Setting up an Amazon FSx for Lustre file system and linking it to the existing Amazon S3 bucket fits this scenario because it provides a high-performance file system optimized for large-scale training workloads.

This architecture can provide sub-millisecond latencies and hundreds of gigabytes per second of aggregate throughput, making it ideal for SageMaker training jobs that must ingest millions of files rapidly.

By linking FSx for Lustre to the S3 bucket, the training job can access S3-backed data through a POSIX-compatible file system interface while benefiting from the throughput and parallelism of FSx for Lustre.

This avoids the performance limitations of repeatedly reading many small files directly from S3 during the training loop.

Exam tip

When a SageMaker scenario mentions millions of files, high-throughput training, and the need to link directly to an existing Amazon S3 bucket with minimal configuration effort, Amazon FSx for Lustre is the correct answer.

Remember:

High-performance training data access from S3: Use Amazon FSx for Lustre.

General shared file storage: Use Amazon EFS.

Simplified S3 access policies: Use S3 access points.

Faster internet uploads to S3: Use S3 Transfer Acceleration.

Official AWS references

What is Amazon FSx for Lustre?

Linking your file system to an S3 bucket

Question 64Skipped

An agricultural technology company, AgroInsight Analytics, manages a data lake using AWS Lake Formation to store and analyze farmer behavior, crop yield records, and agronomic content preferences.

The data lake contains both structured and unstructured data, including field sensor metadata, user activity logs, and clickstream data from the company's digital advisory platform.

The company's data analysts are assigned to specific crop categories, such as cereals, vegetables, or oilseeds. Each analyst needs access to only the data relevant to their assigned category for analysis through Amazon Athena. The company wants to ensure fine-grained access control while reducing operational complexity. 

Which solution will meet these requirements?   
Create separate S3 buckets for each crop category and assign bucket-level permissions to analysts based on their roles.
Explanation
Incorrect. Creating separate S3 buckets per crop category introduces data fragmentation, increases operational overhead for storage management, and does not scale efficiently as the number of categories grows. This bucket-per-category pattern lacks the centralized governance model required and makes cross-category analytics significantly more complex. Keyword tip: separate-bucket-per-domain architectures are considered anti-patterns in AWS data lake design when fine-grained, centralized access control is the primary requirement.
Use AWS Glue workflows to tag data with category-specific tags and manage access using IAM policy conditions based on these tags.
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Glue workflows are orchestration tools for ETL pipelines — they can attach metadata tags to AWS Glue Data Catalog objects, but they do not enforce access control decisions. IAM policy conditions alone cannot interpret Lake Formation LF-Tags for data access enforcement; Lake Formation must be the authority that evaluates and enforces tag-based permissions. Keyword tip: AWS Glue workflows manage data transformation orchestration, not access authorization — do not confuse data cataloging with access enforcement in Lake Formation scenarios.
Correct answer
Use AWS Lake Formation to define permissions based on Lake Formation tags. Assign category-specific tags to the data and associate them with the analysts' IAM roles for fine-grained access control.
Explanation
Correct. AWS Lake Formation tag-based access control (LF-TBAC) is the purpose-built mechanism for enforcing fine-grained, attribute-based permissions in a data lake. By assigning LF-Tags (e.g., category=cereals, category=vegetables) to databases, tables, or columns, and then mapping those tags to IAM roles assigned to analysts, AgroInsight Analytics achieves granular, scalable access control that is consistently enforced across integrated services such as Amazon Athena. This eliminates the need to manage complex, individually maintained S3 bucket policies and scales naturally as new crop categories or analysts are added. Keyword tip: when a question combines 'fine-grained access control,' 'data lake,' 'multiple analyst roles,' and 'reduced operational complexity,' LF-TBAC is the canonical answer.
Use S3 bucket policies to restrict access to folders corresponding to each crop category and configure Athena workgroups for query-level access control.
Explanation
Incorrect. S3 bucket policies operate at the object or prefix level and do not natively enforce fine-grained, query-level access in Amazon Athena. Combining S3 folder-level policies with Athena workgroups introduces significant operational overhead — each new category or analyst requires manual policy updates in multiple places, violating the requirement to reduce operational complexity. Keyword tip: S3 bucket policies are a distractor when the requirement explicitly calls for centralized, fine-grained data lake access control with low operational burden.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of fine-grained access control mechanisms within an AWS Lake Formation-managed data lake.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain of the MLA-C01 exam.

AWS Lake Formation tag-based access control (LF-TBAC) is the recommended approach when multiple principals require attribute-based, row- or column-scoped permissions across a shared data lake.

It provides centralized policy management that propagates consistently to integrated services such as Amazon Athena, AWS Glue, and Amazon Redshift Spectrum.

The concepts at a glance.

    Use AWS Lake Formation to define permissions based on Lake Formation tags. Assign category-specific tags to the data and associate them with the analysts' IAM roles for fine-grained access control: AWS Lake Formation tag-based access control (LF-TBAC) is the purpose-built mechanism for enforcing fine-grained, attribute-based permissions in a data lake.

    Use S3 bucket policies to restrict access to folders corresponding to each crop category and configure Athena workgroups for query-level access control: S3 bucket policies operate at the object or prefix level. They do not natively enforce fine-grained, query-level permissions in Amazon Athena.

    Use AWS Glue workflows to tag data with category-specific tags and manage access using IAM policy conditions based on these tags: AWS Glue workflows are orchestration tools for ETL pipelines. They can attach metadata tags to AWS Glue Data Catalog objects, but they do not enforce Lake Formation access control decisions.

    Create separate S3 buckets for each crop category and assign bucket-level permissions to analysts based on their roles: Creating separate Amazon S3 buckets per crop category introduces data fragmentation and increases operational overhead. This approach does not scale efficiently as the number of crop categories, datasets, and analyst roles grows.

Why AWS Lake Formation tag-based access control fits this scenario.

AWS Lake Formation tag-based access control (LF-TBAC) is the best fit because it enables centralized, fine-grained access control across a shared data lake.

Instead of creating separate S3 buckets, folder structures, or duplicated datasets for each crop category, administrators can assign Lake Formation tags to databases, tables, or columns.

Permissions can then be granted to analyst roles based on those tags.

This creates a scalable access model where access is driven by metadata attributes instead of manually maintained bucket policies.

It also allows consistent governance across services that integrate with Lake Formation, including Amazon Athena, AWS Glue, and Amazon Redshift Spectrum.

This satisfies the scenario’s key requirements:

    Fine-grained access control.

    Multiple analyst roles.

    Centralized data lake governance.

    Reduced operational complexity.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Fine-grained access control + data lake + multiple analyst roles + reduced operational complexity = AWS Lake Formation LF-TBAC.

Remember:

    Attribute-based access control in a data lake: Use AWS Lake Formation LF-TBAC.

    Object or prefix-level S3 access: Use S3 bucket policies.

    ETL orchestration: Use AWS Glue workflows.

    Query execution isolation: Use Athena workgroups.

    Scalable governance across Athena, Glue, and Redshift Spectrum: Use AWS Lake Formation.

Official AWS references.

    What is AWS Lake Formation?

    Tag-based access control in AWS Lake Formation.

Key exam terms: AWS Lake Formation, LF-TBAC, tag-based access control, fine-grained access control, Amazon Athena, IAM roles, data lake governance, reduced operational complexity.

Domain
ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security
Question 65Skipped

You are a machine learning engineer at PixelArena Games, an online gaming company responsible for maintaining a fraud detection model deployed on Amazon SageMaker.

The model processes a high volume of real-time in-game purchase transactions and must respond with low latency to ensure a seamless player experience.
Recently, the model has experienced increased latency during peak gaming hours, and there have been instances where requests were dropped due to insufficient capacity. 

Which approach is the MOST EFFECTIVE for monitoring and resolving these latency and scaling issues in your ML solution? 
Implement a serverless architecture using AWS Lambda for model inference to eliminate latency and scaling issues. Use Amazon CloudFront to cache inference results for faster response times.
Explanation
Incorrect. While AWS Lambda can scale automatically, it is not well-suited for high-throughput, low-latency ML inference workloads that require persistent GPU or CPU instances and consistent compute resources. Additionally, Amazon CloudFront is designed for caching static or semi-static content — it is not an appropriate tool for caching dynamic, per-transaction ML inference responses generated for real-time in-game purchase fraud detection. Keyword tip: when you see 'Lambda for ML inference' combined with 'CloudFront for caching inference results,' recognize this as a distractor that misapplies serverless and CDN patterns to real-time, high-volume ML workloads on SageMaker.
Manually monitor the model's performance using the SageMaker console, and manually increase the instance size whenever latency is detected, to ensure the model remains responsive during peak gaming periods.
Explanation
Incorrect. Manual monitoring and manual instance resizing are operationally inefficient and introduce significant response lag. In a real-time, high-volume gaming transaction environment, the time required for a human operator to detect elevated latency and manually resize instances will result in a degraded player experience and continued dropped requests. The MLA-C01 best practice is to favor automated, event-driven scaling over manual intervention for production SageMaker endpoints. Keyword tip: any answer describing a manual scaling or monitoring process for a real-time, high-volume SageMaker endpoint should be treated as a distractor on the MLA-C01 exam.
Use Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor to track the performance of the model and identify data drift. Adjust the model's hyperparameters based on the results to reduce latency and improve scalability during peak times.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor is specifically designed to detect data drift, model quality degradation, bias, and feature attribution drift over time — not to address infrastructure-level latency or auto-scaling behavior. Furthermore, adjusting hyperparameters is a model training concern that has no effect on endpoint throughput or instance capacity during peak traffic. These are two distinct operational concerns that Model Monitor is not built to resolve. Keyword tip: when the problem involves infrastructure latency and dropped requests — rather than data quality or model accuracy degradation — SageMaker Model Monitor and hyperparameter tuning are the wrong tools.
Correct answer
Configure Amazon CloudWatch to monitor key metrics such as invocation latency, model error rates, and CPU utilization. Set up CloudWatch Alarms to automatically trigger an increase in instance count when latency exceeds a predefined threshold, and enable auto-scaling on the SageMaker endpoint to handle traffic spikes.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon CloudWatch natively integrates with Amazon SageMaker to surface endpoint-level metrics including InvocationsPerInstance, ModelLatency, OverheadLatency, and CPUUtilization. By configuring CloudWatch Alarms on these metrics and linking them to Application Auto Scaling policies for the SageMaker endpoint, the system can automatically provision additional instances when traffic spikes occur during peak gaming hours — addressing both the latency increase and the dropped-request problem without human intervention. This is the recommended MLOps pattern for production SageMaker endpoints. Keyword tip: 'CloudWatch Alarms + SageMaker endpoint auto-scaling' is the canonical solution for reactive infrastructure scaling in MLA-C01 monitoring scenarios involving high latency and dropped requests.
Overall explanation

What this question tests
